PHOTOGRAPHIC APPARAIIIS AD

EXPERIMENTS IN LIGHT VISIBILITY..

-- - MATERIALS OF EVERY DESCRIPTION. LSIKISST STOCK IN TOWN. HAROLD SMITH, rii6TOGRAPHIC CHI4II:ST, 77. BRIL GB ST.. WAJIRINGCON.

The result of the experiments in light vial. bility conducted by Germany and the Nether- lands working in harmony are given as fol- lows: A light of 1 candle-power is plainly visible at one mile, and one of 3 candle-, power at three miles. A 10 candle-power light was seen with a binocular at four miles. one of 29 at five miles, though faintly, and one of 33 candle-power at the same distance! without diffieulty. On an exceptionally clear: .nht a white light of 3-2 candle-power could be distinguished at three miles, one of 5-6 at! IfOur, and one of 17-2 at five miles. The ex-1 ' penments were made with green lights, but .-eecl lights of the same intensity can be seen . at greater distances.

CURIOSITIES OF CHEMICALS.

Certain substances which are deadly in their effects upon men can be taken by the brute creation with impunity. Horses can take large doses of antimony, dogs of mer- cury, goats of tobacco, mice of hemlock, and rabbits of belladonna without injury. On the other hand, dogs and cats are much more susceptible to the influence of chloro- form than man, and are much sooner killed by it. If this invaluable amrsthetie had been tried first upon animals we should probably have never enjoyed its blessings, as it would have been found to be so fatal that its discoverers would have been afraid to test its effects upon human beings. It is evident, then, that an experiment upon an animal can never be the means of any cer- tain deductions so far as man is concerned. No scientist can ever know when trying some new drug or some new oration whether or not when he comes to try it upon man the effect will be the same as that upon animals.

studies.

===== 0002601_19080627_art0029.txt | included=true | included =====
EXPLOSION BY MUSIC.

One of the most dangerous of all explo- sives is a black powder called iodide of nitro- gen. When it is dry the slightest touch will often cause it to expbde with great violence. There appears to be a certain rate of vibra- tion which this compound cannot resist. In experiments to determine the cause of its ex- treme explosiveness, some damp iodide of nitrogen was rubbed on the strings of a bass viol. It is known tat the strings of such an instrument will vibrate when those of a similar instrument,• having an equal ten- sion, are played upon. In this case, after the explosive had become thoroughly dry upon the strings, another bass viol was brought near and the strings sounded. At a certain note the iodide of nitrogen on the prepared instrument exploded. It was found that the explosion occurred only when a rate of vibra- tion of sixty a second was communicated to the prepared .strings. Vibration of the G string caused an explosion, while that of the E string had no effect.

===== 0002601_19080627_art0030.txt | included=true | included =====
EXTRAORDINARY WASTE OF COAL.

If the figures given in an engineering jour- nal as to the consumption of coal on the United States railways are correct, we are confronted with a deplorable state of things, to remedy which the resources of modern science ought to be at qnce set to work. Ac- cording- to these statistics, 150,000,000 tons are used every year. But ot this vast amount only some 7,500,000 tons are actually used in drawing the trains, the remaining 142,500,000 tons, as it is expressed, going up the chim- ney! In other words, being converted into smoke to pollute the atmosphere. CAN SCIENCE REMEDY THIS?

That only some 5 per cent, of the available energy of the fuel can be thus utilised in a modern locomotive is somewhat of a reproach to the science of the century. How is it that a more economical method has not been de- vised? The explanation, perhaps, says the Globe, is that our !meting scientific experts have not turned their attention to such prac- tical and somewhat commonplace problems. The study of radium, the discovery of new elements, and the fascinating problems of electricity have proved more attractive. If, however, the attention of leading men of science was seriously Wined to this ques- tion of the waste of coal, its partial solution at any rate might be quickly achieved: SCIENCE WOULD PROFIT.

And the solution of the problem would-not only be of immense practical importance, it would probably greatly benefit n.ore abstract science in a reflex way. Funds for the ea. , dowment of scientific research are required, ' and are not so freely forthcoming as investi- !ratan' could wish. If science could devise gleans for utilising 20, 30, 4U, or DO per cent. of the energy .of coal, and thus saving many reillions annually, the public would probably be ready to subscribe more liberally for. tls.e endowment of science.

A' TIME" DEVELOPER.

Alfred Watkins, who has done so much to remove the mystery frompposure and development, and to render these processes simple and certhin, has just given a new boon to photographers by introducing a time developer and a patent " therrno " indicator which forms a part of the bottle. A speed list of plates with a relative time indicator, is given with each bottle. The developer, which is concentrated, needs diluting with water, only, then any properly exposed pliVe can be developed with absolute certainty by merely placicg it in the developer or a pre-determined number of minutes. The only variable factor is the temperature at the time of work- ing, and the variation of time for change of tontym- ture is calculated automatically end instantly by the " thertno indicator." There is no acid to watch the plate or to use a dark-room light.

THE DOCTOR'S WORD.

Sir Francis Laking, the King's physician, discourses wisely and gravely -concerning the value of dried currants as a food. Remarkably rich in the saccharine element—profitable to both health and pocket— adding greatly to the nutritive value of bread—an excellent article of food—these are brief extracts from the King's doctor's letters about currants. Your baker is making delicious bread, cakes, buns and scones, all plentifully enriched with currants.

tiLL RIGHTS RESERVED.] A THRILLING MOMENT

By HUME NISBET,

Author of "Bail Up," "The Bushranger's Sweetheart," "The Revenge of /Valerie," "The House of Dread," &e.

There is a fascination and ecstatic sensation In situations of great danger that surpasses all other kinds of pleasure to some natures. The quality of being able to enjoy positively the flick of Death's pinion as he speeds closely by, is not always a sign of courage; although generally the possessors of such nerves are reckoned among the daring children of clay. The mod abject craven may own them as well as the bravest, and enjoy the moment even more acutely by reason of his overmastering terror. While the bravo men and, women can chain their emotions and watch the approaching shadow with guarded if thrilling joy, the weaklings become maniacs, and fling away their chances in their sudden frenzy. The hero stands expectant, quivers as the dark feather brushes him, yet controls his desire. The emotional yields to his delirium and is lost. My friend Graham Travers was a man who courted danger as eagerly as the most ardent lover could a mistress. Born in New Zealand and trained to follow the prudent profession of the law, his wild nature asserted itself early and drove him from his quiet birthplace and peaceful craft to seek adventures wherever such could be found. As a solicitor he was not a success, but as a pioneer and explorer he very soon raade reritAtion.

Ou th--a gold&11.1s of IVestralia he was widely known and respected by his companions for his -fearless pluck and intuitive- skill. Reckless Graham Travers was his title, and when some extraordinary feat had been accomplished it was at once concluded that Graham must have been the hero. Other men might fail and succumb, but Graham always turned up trumps. Hewas always cool and nearly always lucky. Five feet ten- inches in height, with a wiry, agile figure, dark hair, keen blue eyes and a com- plexion which hot suns and icy blaste had raidered indescribable, he made friends wherever It.i went, more with men than with women. Yet the women would have liked him also fully as . . . .

well, if ho had 'courted them half as much as

he did his own sex.

In Westralia, where he lia.d made his first Pile, his marvellous instinct for lopality appeared xlmost supernatwal. Onee, after a banquet at Coolgardie, where he had dined well, if not over wisely, some of his .chums thought to play a joke on him and test this gift. At the close of the feast they placed the inanimate Graham in the 'bottom of the waggonette' where he lay supine on his back. Their intention was to drive towards the desert in the opposite direction .0 Where . they should have gone, and End out if- he could tell where he was when be woke.

But they had not proceeded a mile desert- wards before Graham opened one eye, caught sight of a star, and growled out: "Where ye driving to, ye blame fools! It's the other way to Kalgoorlie." Then he fell asleep again. It was while exploring in New Guinea that he received a copy of the lilac/rated London News, in which a writer ' described the goldfields of Alaska, and while admitting the severity of the climate wrote that he saw no particular hardship to a Briton or a native of any temperate zone. "Of -course," the writer added, it would be utterly impossible for anyone innured to tropical countries to go straight from such a place as New Guinea to Alaska and expect to survive." The writer of this article had unconsciously used the exact bait to hook such a man as Graham Travers. Impossible was a word not in his vocabulary, and he took the words as a direct Challenge. He left New Guinea at once, and dashed over to America by way of San Francisco. Reaching there he found such a rush that there was no hope of a 7.ea passage for months. Then he resolved to go overland. At Vancouver he procured sledges, a portable canoe provisions, dogs /and Indians, and was off to the Araks regions during the worst sensor', and by what was considered an impracticable route. He surmounted the fearsome difficulties, went over great mountains, and had perils and adventures enough to fill a big book. He was snowed up for over two years on the fringe of Mackenzie Bay near where Franklin's party was supposed to have been lost; and although hitherto unused to Arctic travel he displayed his wonderful intuition and acumen in keeping his party and himself alive and in good health. Two of his Indian guides, however, against his advice, left him ,while he was snow-bound and attempted to penetrate the Yukon Valley. They were never heard about after their departure. At last he likewise made the venture, and brought his party, dogs and traps safely to Klondike. Here he examined the fields, report- ing rather unfavourably about them, and reached London the third winter after he had quitted New Guinea., He arrived well, with the exception of an abscess in the right ear, which, however, was quickly cured. Then as the war had commenced in South Africa he went off as a Volunteer to act as private and scout. In Africa he unfortunately, or fortunately, contracted enteric fever near Johannesburg while with. Roberts's Horses and for the time missed any of the _fighting. From 'the hospital he returned to Australia to recruit, and when better rejoined his company, eager to distinguish himself.

Lord Roberts, and afterwards Lord Kitchener, recognised his abilities, and his rise was nearly as startingly rapid as that of 'the soldiers of Napoleon's Army. One letter announced that he had won his stripes, the next that he had been made colour-sergeant. After these each mail Iy:ought news of a promotion, until he had secured a company of his own and the title of Captain Travers. A long pause followed, for he had been sent to an out-of-the-way 'district where Mails were uncertain, and hard work, scanty fare, and constant hunting after a fugitive enemy was the unvarying routine.

The-n tlie welcome peace. was proclaimed, and I met my friend once again home on sick leave. He was then living at a private hotel in Clifton; and over a cigar at my club told me a few of his adventures. These may be related afterwasds ; but I am now going to tell what this man of many hair-breadth escapes in all climes considered to be the most thrilling moment of his life.

The 'environs of Bristol are about the last place in the world where a rover like Graham Travers would look for excitement. Yet at a luxurious and decorously conducted hotel at Clifton, while seeking health, he found more than he had exPected, and likewise lost something vitally important, which he had hitherto carried s tfely through all his wanderings. Captain Travers was not sorry when he learnt that hi a visit to this establishment occurred during one of the quiet seasons. In his present condition he required rest..

The company- was, with but one exception, the hind of people he liked to be with—brotbeo soldiers with their wives or sisters, who wer% able to talk about the war with knowledge and without prejudice; that is men of experience

===== 0002601_19080627_art0039.txt | included=true | included =====

"A- cab was on the road a little distance away, so I hailed it, and getting a jug of water I went hick to Evelyn, and found her.)ust coming round. She had too much vitality to remain long in a fiiut. We got her safely back and up to, her room without being noticed, as the. visitors were dressing for dinner at the time. A wash and a brandy put me to rights, so that I could tell my Story and have dinner with her brother. She didn't appear that night, but next morning came down calm, if a little pale. Of course there was considerable excitement in the hotel over the affair, and the description of my sensations while hanging on, with my enemy lunging at me, gave most of them the thrills." , "It was a thrilling moment and no mistake, Gzaham, old man," I said, as I dipped into my tumbler. [THE END. 3

For the next fortnight Dr. Murphy was con-, fined to his bed, and, as generally the ease is with selfigh ,pcople, the visitors and servants , rejoiced during his absence. ,Oraham Travers made the utmost of this time of peace, and advanced very far along the flowery meads of Paradise. The engagement was now announced, and the marriage settled .to take place a month after the Coronation. Every day, and well-nigh every hour of each day, the lovers were together,. and .the residents had come to regard their being always together as the proper order of affairs. The sick man upstairs was forgotten. On the fifteenth day Dr. Murphy appeared at lunch, with a lurid glitter in his protruding eyes, and hectic spots on his cheeks. He looked as if . . .

he had beer very bad, and ought not to have risen yet, for his legs trembled visibly as he tottered across the dining-hall. There was no disguise about his age now, in spite of hair- , washes and other artificial attempts to cover - . . ,

ROYAL ORDER ANCIENT SHEPHERDS (A,t7.) WARRINGTON DISTRICT. GILBERT GREENALL CHALLENGE CEP.

The members of the Warrington District of the above Order held their annual bowling main on Saturday at the Stag Inn, Walton, in splendid weather. The bowl- ing throughout the main was of a high standard, some exceedingly good games being seen. In the first round a mild sensation was carsed when J. Hussey defeated J. Adams, runner up last year, by 13-10. The second and third rounds saw the chan .1s of some highly fancied candidates rudely shattered— such consistent bowlers as F. Dale, John Lockett, J. T. Harrison and Richard Bennett all receiving their quietus. A notable game in the second round was that between those two stalwarts of Shepherdry, T. Baskerville, sen. • (6). and District Treasurer, James 11).re (5), the former winning by the odd chalk. The Yourth and fifth rounds saw the downfall of two of the Bros. Twiss (Edwin and Herbert), both being easily beaten after having shown previous good form. Another notable game was that between R. Fairbrother cud his son—the elder germ- tion prevailing by 13-9. In the semi-flnal Harry Twigs cracked up very badly, only scoring two chalLs against Walter Leigh, and L. Fairhrother (scratch) had to bow the knee by one point to E. North (4) after a fair exhibition of bowls. The final was a well-contested game. W. -Leigh (I) was the popular fancy, as he had shown exceptional ability all through the main, but his opponent, B. North (4), played an astonishingly good game for one who had previously not done much howling. North held his advantage till the eighth end, when the game was 6 all. From this Leigh took the lead till the twelfth end, when they peelsd at nine. On the next mark North was seen with two in •but his opponent again drew level the following end. The 15th end North chalked another and the. 16th saw birn the winner of the Dreenall Challenge Cup and Councillor J. Fulton Smith's gold medal for 1903. During the afternoon the members sat dona to a substantial tea provided by the landlord (Bro. J. C. Shaw) which gave every satisfaction. The thanks of the officers are due to the supporters of the mein and to those officials who worked so, energetically in carry- ing out the arrangements.

LANCASHIRE EDUCATION COMMITTEE.

Young Children and Secondary Education.

Lancashire Education Cikromittee on 'Monday con- firmed the grants to endowed grammar, schools where children between the ages of eight and twelve wens being educated, notwithstanding a contention that parents of such children, if they desired such instruc- tion, s.hould pay for it thenwelves. It was pointed out that the parents now paid from £4 to £5 per year in such schools.

Archdeacon Fletcher submitted a bulky report upon the scholarships and exhibitions ofTered by the com- mittee, and commented upon the fact that out of 450 exhibitions offered to candidates intending to become teachers they bad only been able to award 173. There had been a fulling oil of over 400 applicants, and only 173 of the candidates had been able to obtain 45 per cent, of the marks. They did not consider it wise to go below this standard of efliciency, believing it would he a waste of public moray to assist them in their education. In the number of candidates for junior exhibitions not limited to teaching there bad been an increase, end out of the money saved above they had awarded an extra number of such exhibitions.

BEAUTIFUL WIERINGTON.

My dear Beautiful Warrington Chillren Last month 1 was invited by one of the branch secretaries to join her and her members in a country ramble. The beautiful trees wore clothing themselves with lovely pale green dresses and attracted a -great deal of attention from the children. We were surprised to fled that the boys and girls were ignorant of the names of even our common British trees. A great writer says:—" A grand tree is like a great poem, not a thing to be glanced at with a thoughtless eye but to be studied. Trees deserve to be learned, and to this end, they should he looked at almost every month in the year, whoa leafless as well as leafly." The pleasant days are here when you lava to ramble in the country. and I want you to have an object in your walks, as aLnless wanderings often lead I to mischief. A naturalist has no idle moments, for when he is not at work in the country, he is busy indoors. If you make little collections of dried plants, insects or fossils, you will fled they are always pretty and provide you with many hours of interest and amusement. I think you are aware that special prizes are bein„a given at the Flower Show for collections of pressed flamers and leaves. The grasses are the best plants to begin with in forming a collec- tion of dried dowers. They are at their beat now and are easily collected, not withering on their way home. And now you want to know hew to dry plants? The method is exceedingly simple. The following iastruc- tions are given by a great naturalist :—" First get together a quantity of old newspapers and fold them to about 18 inches square. Then buy a few sheets of blotting-paper or botanical drying paper, and also 3 or 4 pieces of stout raillboard. Nothing more is wanted. Next we must gather our specimens. Pieces about a foot long are large enough but if the plant be less than 10 or 127 inches in height, it should be taken root and all. Lay one of the boards as a foundation; upon this place a folded newspaper, and upon this a sheet of blotting paper and then the specimen. Over the specimen should come a second sheet of blotting paper, then another newspaper\ and so on until the whole collection is deposited. Place a heavy weight upon the pile, so as to press the plants flat and prevent the air entering to shrivel them. (The easiest weights to use are common bricks.) In this way the pile should be left till the next day, when it should be turned over and the specimens placed into day blotting paper. The pile should be again left to itself for three or four days, when the specimens will be found quite dry. They should then be neatly gummed to sheets of white paper. The name of the plant, and the date and place where gathered should be written underneath."

A collection of wild plants becomes a pleasant re.ninder of happy occasions. Last year I made a collection of sea-weed, and I never look at it without thinking of a happy afternoon spent in gathering it on the shores of the Isle of Alan. But, children, I want to warn you about gathering flowers. Some people think you should not pluck them at all, only look at them and admire them. I would not prevent you from gathering a nosegay to take home to mother, but do not be greedy. Par too often flowers are gathered so lightly that they are carelessly thrown away. Do not spoil t‘rother Nature. Sometimes your visit leaves the country tattered and torn, and soiled beyond recovery. Pluck the flowers so carefully that you do not injure the plant and be satisfied by takingjust enough to serve 3 our purpose. I will close this letter with a-little poem written after the ramble by one of our boy members (Sydney Jeffrey, aged 11 years.)

NATURE. When in the vales and on the hills, We see the streatulet and the rills, We thin' of Natures kindly tonch Of which we often hear so much. She decks with flowers the daisy fields, Which with her loving care she shields. We owe a debt of gratitude To Nature who has been so good. Nature is kind, and everywhere Are tokens of her constant care. Treasure we will wherever found, The gifts that Nature showers around. I am, yours affectionatol 7, V. M. T.

LANCASHIRE FACTORY HAND'S PAIN.

Indigestioa ani 81113nn353 Cirad.

It was in one of the largest cotton mills Preston, situated in new Hall-lane, that Mrs. Hannah Bins, 19, Park-street, Preston, began to be troubled with serious internal pains. " When I began to be ill, now seven years ago," says Mrs. Beggin, " I was dizzy and felt acute pains In my stomach. These almost doubled me up at times and seemed to take all the energy out of me. I went to a well-known Preston doctor, and be gave me two or three bottles of medicine, but these did not (I.) me a bit of good. Indigestion and the puns and depression, grow worse and worse, until I was com- pelled togiva up my work. I got so bad in my head, the pains became so inte..se in my stomach, and t had become so weak I did not seem to have the strength of a baby. Attacks of biliousness came day by day giving me splitting headaches, and shooting pains. "My daughter persuaded me to try Chas. Forde's Bile Beans, which I could soon see were doing me more good than anything else had done. In due time Chas Fettle's Bile Beans completely cured me. I have felt well ever since." Chas. PADGATE.

SCHOLIRS' EXCIIRSION.—A number of the higher standard scholars, of the Padgate C E. School, who wore accompanied by a few adult friends, had a very enjoyable trip to Chester on Saturday. In the forenoon the city walls, the cathedral, and other paints of interest were visited ; while the afternoon was spent in a stroll along the banks of the Dee to Eaton Hell. There the party were enabled to visit the magniticent conserva- tories and gardens, after which a return was made to Cheater by steamer, The weather was ideal and all reached home safely in good time, quite tired out, but with many pleasant recollections. Mums° DAY.—Great preparations are being made for this annual function which takes place to-day, and if the weather is favourable it should pass off with its usual success. The procession will move off at two o'clock: the Church Sunday school leading this year, and being followed by the children from the Cottage homes and the Wesleyan Smaday school. The route taken will be first to Fearnhead, returning thence to Padgate, and proceeding to the finger-post in Padgate - lane, where the procession will turn back. A bait will be made en route and the National Anthem sung. After partaking of tea in their respective schools the children will go to the field for their sports, the Wesleyans going in the field near the railway bridge kindly lent by Mr. William Bennett, and the Church and Industrial children to a field in Green-lane.

The most violent opponent THE WOMEN'S of Women's Suffrage cannot MOVEMENT, deny that the demonstra- tions which have recently taken place in :London have marked a sensible advance in the movement to secure the woman's vote. The great gathering in Hyde Park was impressive for two reasons—first, because is evidenced the strength of the demand; secondly, because it had characteristics which made it dis- tinguished from all other demonstrations which Hyde Park has keown. It was essentially a Woman's Day. The picturesque was called into alliance with the political, and the effect of a great multitude of women, mostly in white, raising a unanimous call for the vote, was admirably engineered from that point of view. But the spectacular display is of secondary importance to the evidence which this demonstration and that of the previous week at the Albert Hall gives of the growth of the movement. Three years ago women politicians were fighting their battle with com- paratively feeble weapons. Here and there one or two of the more militant and courageous ventured to make an appeal to candidates at elections, but their voices had boon disregarded in the clash of more important political issues, and though a few societies existed for the purpose of demanding the franchise they were content to be patronised by a small number of male theorists, or to record peaceful protests against the exclu- sion of the sex. A few people will be found out- side the ranks of the militant suffragists to com- mend the means by which the movement has been forced into its present prominence. Attacks upon the House of Commons, upon Ministers' houses, and upon Ministers themselves at their meetings are not dignified nor par- ticularly ladylike, but it cannot be denied. in the face of what has happened, that the entire- gists themselves gauged fairly well the effect which such tactics would have, and they were certainly justified of themselves in the result. No great reform has ever been accomplished without some measure of violence, and in deciding to throw dignity, and what is more important to the bourgeoisie, gentility, to the winds, the leaders of the suffragists chose a course which was politict.lly sound. Whether we are for or against the grant of the vote to women, it cannot be gainsaid that in two years the movement has made astonishing I progress.

Mr. Asquith's promise that if it were proposed to add a measure of enfranchisement for women to the Reform Bill which the present Government wonld introduce before it goes out of office, the Government will not officially oppose it, is a great P9int gained. But women will be fcolish if they think their work is done because they have centpelled that ccncession frem the Prime Minister, We do not believe that at the present moment there is any real demand on the part of the majority of the women of this country for the vote, and until that can be proved it can hardly be said that so remarkable a change in the con- stitution would be justified. If that majority is to be obtained it will, we believe, be as the result cf a propaganda of persuasion and peaceful argu- ment by women to women, rather than by the Pursuit of such methods as those which have startled politicians and imported a new terror into political life. The attacks upon Ministers, which have hitherto formed the most effective part of the programme of the suffragist Organisa- tions, have done nothing to convince those Who are in theory opposed to the belief that women are capable of undertaking the serious duties and responsibilities of sharing in the Government of the country. Other methods will have to be pursued before the prejudice of many men of capacity and judgment whose Opinions win the respect of their fellows is broken' tlown. The belief that the moral fibre of the nation would be weakened if the supreme decisions of the State were determined by women rests

liPon long-standing tradition and experience. If .t is to be falsified,it must be by the display of that udgmont and restraint on the part of those who advocate reform which has not yet been shown,but which would go far to soften the feeling against the demand.

The Progressive Labour -OPEN-Arn. Pourros. League is keeping abreast of the times and is showing its great usefulness as an asset of the progressive Cause. The past week has been one of vigorous Propaganda in various parts of the town, open-air meetings having been held with the ohjsct of educating the workers on the great questions of the daY and keeping them well-informed as to the achievements of the Government. The members of the League are to be congratulated upon the success ot the campaign' largo audiences having .listened attentively to the homely and well-reasoned addresses, and the general feeling has undoubtedly 13.,,e0n strongly in favour of the Government and of -"Fee Trade. Warringtonians] have been glad to have opportunities of hearing that veteran ,campaigner, Mr. Howes, who has been in his best iorm and has carried his heaters with him in his eXposure of tariff reform fallacies. His illustra- tions of the benefits of the Free Trade on which calr commercial supremacy is based have been Most effective. In his review of the work of the Present administration, he has had no difficulty in Showing that in two sessions they have done More for the well-being of the pe)ple than the previous Tory Governments seem- Plished in twenty years. The Old-Age Pensions Ili 11 is welcomed everywhere by those who are in sYmpathy with the needs of the toiling multitudes, and there is a consensus of opinion that the scheme IS an excellent beginning. The meetings came to a I.2.ttlng close last evening with the visit of Mr. Fred s.laddison, M.P., who always commands attention in Warrington where he is recognized as one of the most powerful speakers of the day on Labour topics. Special interest centred in his visit last evening in view of the vigorous manifasto of indspendence which he and Mr. Henry Vivian,who also well known in Warrington, recently issued. :alr. The proposal that, now that TERRITORIAL the Volunteers have been OFFICERS. transmogrified into Terri- torials, the King shall sanction those who receive commissions using their titles is one which is not likely to have I much effect on recruiting. Hitherto, when a Volunteer officer has been gazetted, and has received his Majesty's comtnission' it has been optional for him to make use of his military I rank when he has pleased. When following his civil occupatim, by common consent lie has I dropped his title, assuming it only when wear- ing the King's uniform. As we are a nation of shopkeepers,the etiquette has laeon appropriate. An efficient Volunteer Co'onel who happens to be a tailor, , a tea-grocer, or a linen-draper gains nothing by flaunting his military rank before customers who, in that respect, 'may be his inferiors. When Edward VIE. was Prince of Wales, a distinguished-looking man who attended a garden-party at Marlborough House caught the eye of his Royal Highness. In private life he was a tailor. His Royal host, remembering his face, desired him to be presented, and then said: " I have seen you before, but cannot recall your name:, In an undertone the visitor replied : "Made your breeches, your Royal Highness." "Au, Major Breeches, I am very glad to see you," responded the Prince of Wales, and from that day the worthy tailor, whose name was Smith or Jones, • was nicknamed "Major Breeches." If Teritorial officers who are engaged in trade assume their titles ander all circurn- tances, it is to be feared that there will be many Major Breeches about. We have happily got over our prejudice against trade ; but, for all that, we like to keep trade and military duty apart, though associated in the same person.

Dr. Canfield, the Librarian SECULAR E UCATION Of Coll.lMbitt University, IN PRACTICE. New York, has the courage of his convictions. his contribution to the discussions of the Pan • Anglican Congress was a paper in which he adva cated frankly the claims of secular over religious or sectarian education. Dr. 043fie1d contended that the secular system had succeeded -in America because it lent itself more to the training of character than any system in which the denom- inations are permitted to compete with each other in the schools. One . advantage which he claimed for it people in this country will appreciate. It had abolished, he declared, sectarian dispotes in villages and towns. That undoubtedly, is a boon, if it is really accomplished ; but we are sceptical as to the actuality of the result. At any rate, competent writers o.p. American social sub- jects do not convey the impression that sectarian disputes are entirely unknown in the United States. As to the effect of the system on the public life of America, without impugning the morality of the nAtion, it is not unjust to observe that the evidence of a high standard of public probity and responsibility as the direct result is not particularly strong. Li this country the secular system, whatever it may have ac aomplished across the Atlantic, would be less a solution than an evasion of the problem; but we should be pre- pared to accept it in order to raise elementary education out of the cock.pit of denominational intolerance and prejudice. It seems to have passed the wit of man to devise an educational scheme which will give each sect equality of oppor- tunity for teaching religion in the schools, and we fail to see why the schools should be used for this purpose. If the Churches will not -agree upon simple Bible teaching, then they must b 3 prepared to see the secular solution in the forefroat of practical politics.

THE VETERANS' FUND.

Exhibition at the Town Hall.

On Tuesday afternoon an exhibition of the articles contributed by the ladies of Warrington and district for the bazaar and fete to be held in London on behalf of the Crimean and Indian Mutiny Veterans' Fund was held at the Town Hall, and was well attended. It was in charge of the Mayoress, who is the lady president of the Warrington Branch, Miss Sylvia Broadbent (hon. secretary), the Misses Smethurst, Mrs. Atkin, Dirs. Lord and Miss M. Broadbent. There was a varied assortment of useful and fancy articles which on Wednesday morning were despatched to the Lady Mayoress of Liverpool who is now in charge of the West Lancashire Stall at the Bazaar, in a motor car kindly lent for the oacasion by Dr. Markel. The Town Clerk has since received a letter from the Hon. Seeretary of the West Lancashire Veterans' Fund conveying the thanks of the Lady Mayoress to i the contributors for their generosity. It s hoped that a substantial sum will be realised at the Bazaar as every town contributing to it will receive a sum from the proceeds proportionate to its contribution. Warrington's contribution, it is expected, will be considerably over £.50. The following is a list of the ladies and gentlemen who contributed articles or money to the Local Bazaar Fund : Mrs. E. EDUCATION COMMITTEE.

Business of tile Next Meeting.

The monthly meeting of the Warrington Education Committee will be held at the Town Hall on Monday evening nest, when the business for consideration will include the following :

ELEMENTS OF HOEST:W/FERY. At a meeting of the School Management Committee the Director was instructed to see if provision could be made for instruction to be given in elements of house- wifery at the Secondary school.

TILE LETTING- OF COUNCIL SCHOOLS. The following resolution passed by the managers of the Beamont Council school was considered viz.:— "That it be a recommendation to the School Manage- ment Committee, as a principle, that the Council schools be not let out of school hours, for other than secular purposes," and, after some discussion, it was resolved that no action be taken in the matter until an application is received for such use of one of the Council schools.

===== 0002601_19080627_art0218.txt | included=true | included =====
PROPOSED NEW scnoor,.

At a meeting of the Sites and Buildings Committee, particulars were submitted of the several sites for schools in the Wellaeld-street'and Lovely-lane districts under the consideration of the committee, and, after some discussion, it was resolved, that a Sub-Committee, consisting of Mr. Councillor John Pemberton, Mr. R. T. Fairclough, and the Chairman of the Committee (Mr. Alderman W. Bolton), be appointed to negotiate with the owners of certain land, with a view to portions of those plots, giving in all an area of not less than 13 acres, being purchased.

_ _ MEDICAL INSPECTION. A meeting took place between the Health Committee of the Council and the Medical Inspection Committee to discuss this question. The Chairman of the Health Committee outlined the proposals of that Committee, after which a discussion took place upon the appoint- ment and duties of the Assistant Medical Officer of Health. A report containing the proposals of the Health Committee for carrying out the inspection was presented, and it was decided to refer it to the Medical Inspection Committee for their further con- sideration and observations.

THE POLITICS OF THE WEEK.

[BY OUR LONDON CORRESPONDENT.]

The social legislation of the Government has come into great prominence this week, and in connection with this it is important to note the &finite aim of the Government as set forth by the Prime Minister himself in his Birmingham speech at the National Liberal Federation meetings. A PEOPLE'S CHARTER.

The Prime Minister vras meeting Vie cheap and common sneer of his party opponents that he was angling for votes by his measures. Ile therefore made an emplicit statement of the object of Liberal policy, which amounts to a new People's Charter. Mr. Asquith is a master of language, and his party have to thank him for this formula, wnich will take its place in the historic utterances of Liberalism : "To diffuse and, so far as may be, to equalise the burdens, to make life brighter, easier, richer, more fruitful for the bulk of our fellow-countrymen, to break through and to break down the preseat environment of squalor and of temptation, and to banish, if we can, and in so far as we can, the shadow of anxiety for the future which to so many of them, darkens at every stage the Journey from youth to old age." That, the Prime Minister adds, is, in the belief of the Liberals, "the only way in which to make ths best of each in the interests of all—to create and sustain, in the only worthy sense of the word, a community of free men in a free State."

===== 0002601_19080627_art0223.txt | included=true | included =====
THE OPPOSiTION AND THE MINERS.

Theoretically, everybody accepts the view that if men's lives are to be made brighter and easier," the workers in the mines have a claim to early considera- tion; and for the last two years there has been practical agreement among l parties that if the miners themselves could settle their differences, an eight hours day should be established by some less -disastrous method than an industrial strike. The minority against the bills for eight hours in mines had been wiped out. The Liberal Ministry was driven to deal with the subject. They had a mandate not only from the country, but from the House itself. But now that they have taken it up, as no Government could possibly have avoided taking it up, the situation is marvellously altered. They have now to face an organized Tory opposition. It began some timago at the by-elections, when a mysterious body called the Coal Consumers' Association appeared on die electoral scene. We live in times when political disguises are worn, and have to be penetrated in order to get a true view of things. We have to look for Tories and Protectionists under the coats of "Reformers," and under the hats of the majority of the Coal Consumers are likely to be found Tory manufacturers and ship- owners. At all events, the hostility of the Opposition to the Eight Hours Bill for Miners, which has so unexpectedly developed, iu mainly inspired by these people.

===== 0002601_19080627_art0224.txt | included=true | included =====
FEARS OF THE EIGHT HOURS BILL.

It is fair to note that there is some opposition to the Miners' Bill on the Liberal side also, and that the Chambers of Commerce, which are not all composed of Tor? manufacturers, were induced to pass a hostile resolution on the eve of the second reading debate. The animating motive with these, who fear the Bill and oppose it from a non-party point of view, is a belief that the economic results of the measure will be disastrous—that is to say, that there will be a great contraction in the output of coal, a corresponding increase in price, and a consequent partial paralysis of industry. The view of the Government is that these views are not justified ; that in so far as they may be entertained they should be calmed by the modifications made in the Bill to bring about the change gradually; and that the miners themselves i are co-operating n the desire to prevent any sudden dislocation of trade. Mr. Russell Rea, the Chairman of the Committee which inquired into the probable economic effects of the measure, spoke in the debate, and nothing could be clearer than his concluding assurances that he would not be surprised if there was no increase at all in the price—of coal, and kat it was only during the period of transition that there could be any disturbance of equilibrium. The fact that the coal-owners bad said that they preferred a change once for all to a gradual one, threw some doubt on the validity of the grounds of their appre- hensions. THE VIEW OF THE MITERS.

The House of Commons is now rich in representa- tion of Labour, and especially in members who have personal experieace of the mines. Their testimony was exceedingly conAdent and reassuring as to the effect of thd Bill on the output. They frankly ridiculed fear—and on this point they ought to know. From their standpoint the Prime Minister's view that these ameliorations of the lot of the worker are not only to make lifa brighter and easier, but "more fruitful," will be realised. Not less work will be clone in the shorter time, but possibly more. Work will be brisker while it lasts. The miners won't want so much " play " time. It is folly to think that continuous work- in cramped positions and in the unhealthy con- ditions of the dark mines can be done for nine or ten hours. Mr. Enoch Edwards puts it that the miner might pass the time there for nine hours, but he could not work all that time. As to price of coal, the miner cannot control it ; and the debate, letting the public know, as it does, what the coal costs at the pit-mouth, raises questions as to whether the humbler class of coal-consumer would ever blame the easier lot of the miner for having to pay more. What the miners claim is a share in the brighter life promised to work-people by all parties, who for years have been taunting each other with not getting on with social reforms. They are "looking to this Parliament " the phrase is that of of Mr. Enoch Edwards—" for more tolerable conditions of life." AGE PENSION SLIDING-SCALE.

The first' night of the Committee debate on the Age Pension Bill was remarkable for two things the waste of time on petty points by Tories who are at the. heart opposed to the Bill, and the acceptance by the Government of a sliding-scale instead of a fixed pension and fixed income limit. Here, also,-there was a disappointment to the Labour members, who, how- ever, are, like other people, divided on the subject. The trouble arises through the want of money to go further than the Government announced, or at least much further. If the ministers could at one and the same time keep the limit of the pensioner's own income at 10s., and have a sliding-scale beginning at that figure, all would be well ; for then the Friendly Societies whose members can earn more than 10s. superannuation would not lose their pension. The man who had lls, of his own would get 45., if he had 129. he would get 3s. and so on, and he would not be entirely without Claim to a State pension of between Is. and ss. unless he had 15s. of his own. This year, however, the Chancellor of the Exchequer's resources will not permit of this. If he puts on a sliding-scale he must shift the income limit a little lower, so that the graduation begins from the man who has 85. The Chancellor of the Exchequer has postponed the consideration of his schedule, and nobody knows exactly how it will eventually work out; but it means that the people with 10s. who expected te receive ss. pension, will probably get only 3s. for the present ; and, on the other hand, pensions of smaller amount will be payable until the new income limit of 13s. is reached. _ .

In the debate on Wednesday, the Chancellor of the Exchequer, Mr. Lloyd George, accepted an amend- ment that married couples over 70 should each be entitled to the full pension, instead of suffering any disability by reason of their being married and living together. This is a most valuable concession, for which the government will be thanked by many a Darby and Joan. The House was unanimous in favour of the amendment, and the Chancellor did well to meet the sentiment in a generous spirit though the cost of the pensions will, of course, be somewhat increased.

===== 0002601_19080627_art0225.txt | included=true | included =====
PUDSEY ELECTION.

The result of the election at Pudsey, giving a seat to the Opposition as the clear result of the Socialist candidature, ought to settle the question of the necessity for a second ballot, or alternative vote. The new Tory member does not represent half the voters,. and it cannot be doubted that Mr. Ogden would have been M.P. for Pudsey if there had been a second election, or a recount of votes as between him and Mr. Oddy, under what is known as the second or alterna- tive vote. But another question is brought into prominence by this election ; and that is, the right of the freehold voters of Leeds to turn the scale of an election at Pridsey. Had the Plural Voting Bill become law, the result would have been different ; even if it had n3t been different it would have been more just.

The presentation took the form of an album, containing a testimonial, with a list of contributors, a cheque, and an easy chair, and to Mrs. Morton a silver rose bowl

WALKING DAY.

Preparations for Next Friday's Festival.

Friday next is Walking Day, and the preparations for the great children's festival are practically com- pleted. Everyone is looking forward to the events of the day with pleasure, and given genial weather, the youngsters are sure to enjoy themselves right merrily. From an early hour the streets will present an animated scene. Then the processions bright with banners, and headed by bands discoursing lively airs, will move on their way to the lawn facing the Town Hall —the place of assembly of the Chnrcia of England and Nonconformist schools. Promptly at ten o'clock the signal will be given for the Blue Coat Sahool —who always have the place of honour —to lead off the pro- cession through the centre arch. As usual the soh )ols will leave in the following order : (1) Blue Coat School and Band' (2) Padgate Industrial School and Band (3) Parish Church and St. Helen's Churcli Hollinfare (4) St. George's Mission (5) St. Clement's Mission (0) St. Peter's School (7,) St. Paul's (8) All Saints' Mission (9) St. Ann's (10) Holy Trinity (11) St. Luke's (12) Factory Yard Mission (13) St. Barnabas' (14) St. James', Latchford (15) Emmanuel Reformed Church (10) Buckley Street (17) Salvation Army (18) Ragged School The usual route —Arpley-street, Wilson-Patten- street and Bridge-street--will be followed. The Roman Catholic procession will take place immediately after the Church of England and Nonconformists have passed along the line of route, and promises to equal if not excel in brilliance any previous enterprises of the kind. Large banners of exquisite texture and workmanship will again be a leading feature. The procession will be seen in its entirety in Bridge-street. In the afternoon there will be an invasion of fields in all parts of the district, where the youngsters will be able to romp to their hearts' content. Following the example of previoue years most of the Nonconformist schools will content themselves with a procession along a short route of their own, the intention being to give the children a complete day's enjoyment in the country.

Walking Day at Stockton Heath. Stockton Heath always has a Walking Day of its own and the festival was successfully celebrated yesterday. At noon, when the children were busily preparing for the procession, the outlook was threaten- ing ; but the clouds rolled away and there was no rain to mar the enjoyment. Shortly before two o'clock a brief service was held in the Church and afterwards the scholars paraded the village. As in the past the band of the Blue Coat School took the lead and enlivened the proceedings with spirited marches. The clery and officers of St. Thomas' Church followed and theii came a detachment of the Church Lads' Brigade looking very smart and trim in their uniforms. As usual there was a brave display of flags and some of the children carried baskets of flowers. The infants were accommodated on gaily-bedecked lurries and appeared to enjoy themselves as well as their seniors. After the procession tea was served in the schools and the Church Institute, and later the scholars betook themselves to the Wilderspool Brewery Park where the usual sports were engaged in.

. NEW BRIGHTON TOWER.

Tne management of New Brighton Tower are making as strong efforts in the matter of popularising their Sunday concerts as they are to gain public favour for the Tower and Park as an amusement resort. To this end they have made arrangements with some of the most eminent vocalists and instrumentalists in England, and these will appear from time to time in the Sunday concerts at the Tower, which Sunday concerts are the only moans of entertainment offered to the people of Liverpool and district on Sabbath evenings. For Sunday, June 28th, the most famous and popular of all English speaking prima donnas, Madlle Zelie de Lussnn will be the vocalist. Miss de Lussan has always been a prime favourite with Covent Garden audiences, and as the principal star of the Moody Manners Opera Company, and the Carl Rosa Opera Company, she by her wonderful abilities, took high rank in the affections of the British public. Her appearance at New Brighton Tower on Sunday night is sure to bø attended by large audiences. The management of the Tower have during the past week added a large number of animals to their free menagerie. They are :-1 brown bear, 2 leopards, 2 cashmere goats, 6 American marmots, 2 buffaloes, 1 cassowary, 1 camel, 2 yaks, 2 hyaenas, and 2 wolves.

ITAILOR-MADE CYCLE BREECHES from 6s, 11d.— BA.NXS, Outfitter, Horsemarket-st.—Advt. , Co-operative Employees' Picnic. I On Thursday the employees of the Warrington Co-operative Society and their wives and friends had their annual picnic, Blackpool being the resort visited. The party, numbering over 100, left War- rington at 7.50 and arriving at Blackpool in good time spent a long day in sampling the various indoor and outdoor amusements, for which Blackpool is so I widely famed. The weather was beautifully fine, and ' the outing was voted a great success. Mr. Atkinson (manager), and Mr. Jarman (secretary) accompanied the party. Mr. J. C. Sutton's Retirement. To mark their appreciation of his long and faithful s services, the Guardians of the Warrington Union have decided to present their retiring clerk, Mr. J. C. Sutton, with a portrait of himself. The work was ' entrusted to Mr. T. Birtles, Sankey-street, who has completed a fine large portrait in carbon, and this is now on view in the window of his studio. It is an • excellent likeness, and forms a very suitable gift. A I replica will be hung in the Board Room at the I Workhouse. Salt Trade Slacker. ,(13,. Although the prices of salt are well maintained, the white and rock salt exported from Cheshire last month, was only 56,129 tons, a decrease of over 8,000 tons on the corresponding period last year Liverpoil shipments showed the falling off, Runcorn land the Ship Canal enjoying increased shipments of 4,000 tons. The month's depression was due to the decrease of 4,000 tons to Asia, nearly 5,000 tons to North America, and 1,700 tons to th 4 United States. The coast trade was better. I At the Warrington Police Court on Monday, Edmund IGriffo, Adamson-street, Ashton-in-Makerfield, was charged with embezzling the sum of £1 belonging to the Hygienic Tooth Company, Warrington, on January Bth.—Mr. Steel, solicitor, appeared on behalf of the prisoner and pleaded guilty. He said there were three charges against Griffo, but the prosecutors • had no desire to be vindictive and had withdrawn the other two on prisoner agreeing to plead guilty to this aharge.—Mr. Lindsay, solicitor, of Liverpool, said the prosecutors would not like to see prisoner sent to gaol but they wished to make an example of him, as they had many men in their employ. They would esteem i it a favour if the Bench bound the prisoner over.—The IMayor said the prisoner ought to be thankful for the ' mercy shown to him by the prosecutors. He would be bound over under the Probation of Offenders Act for six months and would have to pay the costs. For Sale, cheap, 2Sinch GREEN'S PONY LAWN MOWER, good condition, suit cricket club.— GARLAND, 51, Bridge et., Warrington.—Advt. Sankey Bridges Wesley Guild. On Saturday afternoon the members of the Sankey Bridges Wesley Guild, to the number of 50, had a very enjoyable ramble. Some went by ear to Stockton Heath, but the greater number walked the whcle ' distance. A (ter passing Victoria-square, they walked up London Bridge, and then along the footpath past Broom Cottage to Appleton Dingle. The country was at its best and the scenery was much admired by all .the picnicers. They walked to Appleton Thorn, and for about two hours they induleed in games in a field kindly lent by Mr. Poi-slow. The return journey was taken along a very rustic footpath which leaves the road near the Cross, and comes into the Lumbrook- road just above the Diogle. 'On reaching Stockton Beath via Lumbrook, the cars were boarded, and Warrington was reached about nine o'clock in the evening after a splendid afternoon's walk. The members of the Sankey Bridges Wesley Guild Cycle Club had a run to Arley on Saturday, and spent the afternoon in that picturesque neighbourhood. The outing was thoroughly enjoyed, and Warrington was reached about nine o'clock. PUBLIC BENEFIT—UNDER Tall CLOCK on BEST BOWS_ IN LANCASIIIRE.—AdVt. _

The Late Mrs. H. Collin. Much sympathy has been expressed with Mr. H. Collin, of Marson street, on the death of his wife, who passed away on Wednesday of last week. The funeral took place at the Cemetery on Saturday afternoon in the presence of a large gathering. At 1.15 a ser- vice was held in St. Paul's Church, which the deceased lady bed been in the habit of attending, and here a large congregation had assembled. The funeral cortege was met at the church gates by the Revs. W. Bracecamp (vicar) and E. E. Peters and J. H. Bankes (curates), Mr. Peters reading the first part of the service outside,- while as the procession wended its way up the aisle the organist (Mr. Wilkes) played "I know that my Redeemer liveth." The remainder of the service was taken by the Vicar, who also spoke in high terms of the deceased. The hymns sung were" 0 God, our help in ages past" " On the resurrection morning," and " Brief life is here our portion." The cortege then left for the Cemetery, the principal mourners being Mr. H. Collin (the husband) Miss Collin, Miss A Collin and Master Jack Collin, Mr. James Bowcock, Mr. George Bow- cock, Mr. John Wainwright, Mr. W. Gandy, Mr. E. Ankland, jun., Mr. W. Leigh, Mr. James Parkinson, Mr. Harold Houghton, Mr. Josiah Wainwright. Mr. Ernest White and Mr. James Warburton.

RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

CHEMICAL WORKS FIRE.

At Runcorn, on Monday morning, a fire occurred at the carbon plant of Wiggs works of the United Alkali Company. The flames shot about thirty feet high. The material is very explosive, but there is a very efficient fire brigade and apparatus, and there was only one slight explosion. The are was got under in an hour. The damage is considerable. CYCLIST'S SHOOTING. EXTRAORDINARY CONDUCT.

Extraordinary conduct by a cyclist was disclosed at Frodsham Petty Sessions, on Wedueslay.-13arold Aldrid of Farnworth, Bolton, was summoned for firing a rsvolver on the highway at Dunham Hill. William Booth, a Liverpool cyclist, said be was resting on the roadside when there was the report of a firearm, and he was struck on the face. Aldrid shouted," Hello! Have I shot you?" After local attention Booth proceeded •to the Liverpool Royallntlrmary, where the bullet was extracted from above his jaw.—Dafendant said he shot at a milestone, and the bullet ricocheted and struek Booth.—Defendant was fined 40s. and costs. BROTHER'S BRAVERY REWARDED.

Before the usual business commenced at the Widnes Police Court on Monday the chairman (Councillor F. Nield) presented to Peter Sutton, a well-known mem- ber of the Widnes Svvitaming Club, a silver medal and certificate of the Liverpool Shipwreck and Humane Society for saving his brother's life on April 13th. On that date there was a boatrace on the Mersey from Widnes to 'Warrington. Sutton and his brother were in one of the boats. The reuipient had charge of the tiller, and the other brother, who had charge of the sails, was blown overboard. Peter plunged in, although a strong tido was mania''', and brought his brother to the side of the boat. The chairman dwelt strongly upon this noble act, and alluded to the provision made by- the education authorities to promote the art amongst the schoolboys and girls.

RUNCORN BOARD OF GUARDIANS. The fortnightly meeting of this Board was held at Dalton Workhouse on Tuesday, SIN T. Baxter pre- sidium. There were also present :—Messrs. G. Pilling, S. Woodward, T. Wright, A. R.Normsn, W. :%I.Nforris, J. Douglas, P. Collins, E.Britch, G.Egerton-Warburtou, P. Booth, C. E. Linker, C. E. Liii_iker, jun., J. IL Davies S. Woodward, W. Harrison, J. dallows, J. L. Jono3, b. Horton, J. Spruce, W. Winstantey, Admlrat J. P. Jones Parry, J. B. Chadwick, A. Ilaining, G. F. Ashton (clerk), W. E. Hough (claputy clerk), T. It. Rollinson (master), T. Cook, F. Pearson and W. R. dePrez (relieving °Ulcers). OLD AGE PENSIONS AND OUT Itra.TEr. . . . . .

The Clerk read the following resolution wlich had been adopted and forwarded by the York Board of Guartians: "That the York Out-Relief Union regrets ' that in the contemplated provision for old age ponsions deserving persons over 70 years of ago in receipt of relief are to be excluded. This Union hopes that tho proposals may be so amended as to allow such recipants of relief the choice of transfer from poor relief to pension, thus removing the stigma of pauperism and placing them on an equality with others receiving old age pensions." The Board was asked to pass a similar resolution and forward it to the Local Government Board and the members of Parliament representing the Union.

Mr. Morris said they ought to support the resolution. It touched one of the weak points in the Bill, that a person in receipt of out-door relief was debarred, no matter how deserving he was. He moved the adoption of the resolution. Mr. C. E. Linaker said it was not quite clear how the Act operated. He had seen it stated that anybody in receipt of relief on the Ist January, 1909 would not be eligible, and also that anybody ia receipt of relief on the Ist January, 1909, would not be eligible. If the qualifying date was January Ist, 1909, they could take off the names of those receiving relief the last week in December and then they would become qualified. Mr. Hallows seconded. The Board ought to render all the help they could. A person might be receiving 35. per week from the rates and another person next door a° more deserving, might be getting an old-age pension. Old-age pensions were no new thing. For many years people who had been getting big salaries hal also been granted princely pensions but when the workingman asked for a pension they held up their hands in pious horror. The vvorkingatau did not ask for £lO a week but only ss. Mr. Linaker said it was clear that the object was to prevent old-age pensions taking the place of relief. Mr. Pilling remarked that he quite agreed the wrong people had been getting pensions. (Laughter.) Mr. Norman said the question of the Poor Law was also being dealt with and that might alter the condition of affairs very considerably. The motion was agreed to. COUNCY COUNCIL EXPENDITURE. The Clerk stated that the following resolutions had been adopted and forwarded by the Bucklow Board of Guardians :—Thar this Board strongly protests against the ever-increasing demands of the Cheshire County Council in calling for county and other rates so largely in excess cf the amount which the Guardians were led to believe would be required when framing their esti- mates for the cumin; half-year, such excess amount in this Union being no less a sum than £2,318 odd which to enable the Guardians to meet will necessitate an additional call upon every parish of the Union. (2) That this Board also protests against the practice adopted by the Cheshire County Council in calling for a larger sum than will be necessary to meet its imme- diate requirements, e.g., in asking for £2,301 on account of general higher education when they have a surplus fund of £20,000. Mr. Wright moved that the Board support the resolu- tion. The County Council hardly seemed to know what they wanted. Fortunately the Rnncorn Board had some money in hand and it was not necessary for them to levy a supplementary rate. Mr. Hallows said he had been speaking to a County Councillor who told him that he thanked the Rancorn Board for the remarks made about County Council expenditure at the last meeting. He said that those who were trying to keep down expenditure were in a minority but soch discussions would help them. Mr. Woodward: Which remarks do you mean? There were remarks on both 'sides orthe question. Mr. Hallows : On the right side—that of the hampered ratepayers. The resolution was carried. Mr. Linaker: lam glad the Board sees the light of day at last. FINANCE. - .

Mr. Woodward reported that there was a balance in the treasurer's hands of £3,795 12s. sd.

INCREASE OF VAGRANCY. Mr. Spruce said there had been a large increase in the number of vagrants received at the House during the fortnight. The number was 201 as compared with 149 for the corresponding period last year. The number of inmates of the House was 152 as compared with 143. Mr. Woodward liaid he was not surprised at the increase in the number of vagrants as the weather had been so favourable.

A CONTRACTOR'S LOSS. Mr. Thomas Kilburn,contractor, Bolton, who con- structed the Walton Inferior Sewerage Works,intimated that he had sustained a loss of about £250 mainly on the flushing tanks of which he had no previous experi- ence and he asked for a grant towards his loss. The Cler said that if the Council acknowledged liability to every contractor who made a loss it would probably become a heavy matter. The letter was referred to the Walton Sewerage Committee.

RE PREEAUTIONS.

The following letter from Mr. J. J. Shaw, secretary of the Runcorn and Widnes Co-operative Society, addressed to the Clerk as Clerk to the Weston, Halton, Frodsham, and lielsby Parish Council, was read :My Board have recently been in communication with the Runcorn Urban Council with regard to the services of the Runcorn Fire Brigade and appliances in case of out- break in the above parishes in each of which we are !arse owners of property. We are informed that the Brigade is to confine its services to the Urban district and there appears to be no means provided for coping with any outbreaks in the above mentioned districts. The position is, therefore, a very serious one, and I am desired to suggest that your parish councils should give the matter their early consideration, and either by arrangement with the Runcorn Urban Council or other- wise make provision for minimising the grave danger to property and life which exists at present in the parishes mentioned. Mr. Linaker said Frodsham and Holsby were properly provided for. He did not think it would be much use making terms with the Urban District Council with their present appliances. They were entirely out of date, lie moved that the Clerk reply to the society suggesting that they should ascertain the pressure on the hydrants to see if the hoses supplied to each township would be sufficient. He believed they would be quite as good as the Runcorn are engine, with all due respect to it. (Laughter.) This course was agreed to. FINANCIAL. Mr. Woodward reported that the balance in the hands of the treasurer amounted to £5887 Is. 2d.

MATADOR TICE DISTRICT. Dr. Adams reported two cases of scarlet fever at Weston and three at Halton. He also stated that whoopinF, cough and measles were very prevalent in the district.

ALLEGED AITENIPTED SUICIDE AT R UNCORN

On Wednesday, at Runcorn, William Keogh, a young bricksetter, was brought from the hospital to the police station on a charge of attempting to commit suicide by cutting his throat and stabbing himself in the abdomen. Prisoner rose from bed, and in the kitchen be picked up a table knife worn to a point. His uncle, who followed, had a fearful struggle with him. Prisoner had lately been strange. He was remanded for a week.

The September meeting was -flxecl to take place at Earlestowa. CONFIRMATION.

A confirmation service was held in St. John's Parish Church on Monday night, at eight o'clock by the Bishop of Liverpool (Dr. Chavasso), who motored from Liverpool, accompanied by Mrs. Chavasse. There were 74 candidates, which constitutes a record in the parish for one year, the service being now held yearly instead or every two years. Tne candidates had been prepated in their duties by the Vicar (ttev. V. Et. Matthews) mil his curate (Dr. Devinny). Oa assembling at h e church the female candidates were assisted by Mrs. Wilson, the Vicar's daughter, in preparing for the service. The Bishop, in his address, appealed to the candidates to strictly adhere to their vows, and walk in the straight path of purity and goodness. Special hymns were rendered by the choir.

POSTAL AND SAVINGS BANK REGULATIONS

For further particulars see "Post 011 ice Guide," at any Post alic4. Posia OE STAMPS. Books oontainitig 12 penny and 23 halfpenny stamps are now on sale tit oach post °face. Price 2s. INLAND CORRESPONDENCPL

The term Inland Correspondence " Means cor- respondence posted within the United Kingdom, including the Isle of Man and the Channel Islands. "Inland Coruspondence " is divided into the following classes.: (a) Letters (d) Newspapers (b) Postcards (e) Parcels (c) Halfpenny packets Not only letters, but any document or article (with a few trifling exceptions) which can be trans- mitted by post at all, may be sent by letter poet, the only limitation being that of size. LEITER POST. The prepaid rate of postage is as follows Not exceeding 4 OM in weight ... Id. For every additional 2 0z5.... id. A letter posted unpaid is chargeable on delivery with double postage; a letter insufilaiently paid, with double the deficiency. No letter may exceed two feet in length, one foot in width, or one foot in depth.

POST-CARDS. The prepaid rate of postage on every post-card is one half-penny, and on every reply post-card one penny. A post-card posted unpaid is chargeable on delivery with one penny. Cards used as post-cards must be mace of ordinary card-board, not thicker than tho material used for the thickest official post-card. They may not exceed 5} inches in length by 3} inclies in width, or be less than 3} inches in length by 21 inches in width. A gummed label (not to exceed 2 inches long and -inch wide) bearing the address may now be affixed to the address side of card. If soy of the roles relating to a post-card be infringed, the card will be treated as a letter. HALFPENNY PACKET POST.

..—.—.- . The Halfpenny Packet Post can be used only for packets not exceeding 2oz. in weight. Printed matter, &c., tot in the nature of a letter, may be transmitted by this method. NEWSPAPER POST. .

The prepaid rate of postage on a registered news- paper, including any supplement thereto, whether posted singly or with others is id. Newspapers not registerad at the General Post Office are treated in the pcst like any other printed matter.

PARCEL POST. The rates of postage on parcels areas follows: Fora parcel ... ... not exceeding 1 lb. 0 3 exceeding 1 lb. 2 lb. 0 4 tt , ff PP „ 2 lb. „ 3,1 b. 0 5 ff „ 3 lb. f 1 5 lb. 0 6 f f „ 5 lb. e 7 lb. 0 7 t, „ 7 lb. f 1 8 lb. 0 8 ff PP 8 lb. ff 9 lb. 0 9 If 11 If 9 lb. 10 lb. 010 „ 10 lb. If 11 lb. 011 ff No parcel may exceed 11th. in weight. The size allowed for an inland parcel la : Greatestlength... ... ... ... 3 ft. 6 in. Greatest length and girth combined 6 ft. 0 in. For example—A parcel measuring Sft. Gin. in its longest dimensions as much as 2ft. Gin, round its thiskesi part ; a short parcel may be thicker; thus, If it measure no more than 3ft. in length, it may measure as much as 3ft. round its thickest part. The postage on a parcel must be prepaid. Mark each parcel" Parcel Post." A parcel must not be posted in a letter box, but presented at the counter of a post office, and the sender should see that the weight, size and postage are in order before leaving. PARCELS (FOREIGN AND COLONIAL). Parcels are accepted for parcel post to most foreign countries under conditions similar to the inland regulations, except that for customs require- ments the contents must be declared. The customs charges can be prepaid or collected on delivery. The postage varies to different countries and must be prepaid. . MONEY ORDERS (INLAND.) The postal authorities cannot issue a single money order for more than £4O, and will only cash them at the money order ofilce at which they are made payable, unless they are crossed, when they can be paid through a bank. No fractional part of a id. can be contnined in a money order. The rates of poundage are :—Not exceeding £l, 23. ; £3, 3d.; £lO, 4d.; £2O, 63. ; £3O, Bd. ; £4O, Io,i. By paying an additional 2d. and cost of the telegram of advice, n money order can be telegraphed under certain restrictions. MONEY ORDERS (FOREIGN AND COLONIAL.) The maximum amount for which a money order i may be issued for places abroad varies n each particular place. The poundage rates are :—Not exceeding £l, 3d. ; £2, Gd. ; and above that, 3d. for each additional £2 or portion of it. A foe of Gd. in addition to the ordinary poundage, together with cost of telegram of advice, enables money orders to be telegraphed to certain places abroad. POSTAL ORDERS. -

These are obtainable at any money order office in the United Kingdom, and possess the advantage over money orders that they can be cashed at any money order office. If crossed, they can be paid into a bank. They are issued for fixed amounts, rising by ed., from ed. to .R 1 Is. 'The poundage fees are up to 2s. 6d.; Id. up to 155.; lid. up to 21s. Before parting with a postal order, always fill in the name of the person to whom the amount is to be paid. Each order is numbered, as is also the counterfoil attached, which latter should be retained and name of payee filled in. The Post- master-General will entertain ma further claim after an order is once paid. Stamps to the amount of sd, can be affixed to make up odd sums. TELEGRAMS (INLAND). The Postmaster-General possesses,al most entirely, the privilege of transmitting,colleming and deliver- ing telegrams in the United Kingdom. The ehargo for a telegram is a minimum amount of ed. which covers 12 words, and an additional td. /or each extra word. This charge includes delivery within the postal district if sent out for delivery from a head telegraph office, but if from a sub-office then within three miles only, beyond which a charge of 3d. per mile is made from the office door. All charges are, as far as possible, prepared by affixing stamps to telegram form. Address of person receiv- ing a telegram has to be paid for, \ but sender's name if written on back of form need not be. Five figures count as one word. The cost of a reply may be prepaid. Telegrams can be repeated on payment of half the original charge, which extra payment • is refunded if error is detected. Telegrams may be posted in wall Or pillar boxes, or handed to rural postmen, and are wired as soon as received at the nearest telegraph office. Late fees secure attention, in certain easel, to telegrams handed in after the usual hours. The cost of registering an abbrevi- ated addresq, which must consist of two words, is RI is.

--• TELE OTIAMS (FOREIGN AND COLONIAL) Must be written on special forms and are accepted In the language of any of the principal European countries. The rates vary, and are too numerous to detail here. POST OFFICE SAVINGS BANK.

These are established at all money order offices. In ordinary cases the annual deposit must not exceed £5O, and the total amount £2OO. No person can have more than one account. Amounts placed in the bank on behalf of children under seven years of age cannot be withdrawn until the child reaches that age, and then only under his or her own signa- ture. The smallest amount received at a time is Is., and this may take the form of 12 penny stamps affixed to a special form. Each depositor receives a bank book in which the =mint of each deposit should be entered at the time of paying in. If the deposit is £1 or over, a spacial acknowledgment is sent from the heart London office. Interest is

allowed at the rate of 2 per cent. Any amount up to and including tiny be withdrawn without previous notice, on presenting a bank book. The bank book should be sent each year, postage free, on the anniversary of the date when the account was opened, to the Controller of the Savings Bank Department for purposes of checking and adding interest. The withdrawal of money can be expedited by telegraphing, the depositor paying cost of telegrams. P.O. TELEPHONE AND TRUNK LINE CfIARGE9. The fees for the use of the Trunk lines are .as follows:— For 25 miles or under ... 3d. ff 50 If I 75 If If „ 100 „ „ " every additional 40 miles or fraction thereof... _ 61.

...... Between the hours of 7 p.m. and 7 a.m., sis minates conversstlon is allowed for the same price as three minutes between 7 a.m. and 7 p.m., and a reduced charge is made for three minute calls.

MOTOR CAR_ AND CVCLE_ REGULATIONS.

Each one must be registered with the council of a county or county borough, the fee being .E 1 for a motor car, and ss. for a motor cycle. A registered number is assigned to each, which it is an offence not to keep affixed when driving. •Esch driver must possess a license, costing 55., to be renewed every 12 months, and obtainable from the county authorities, which mny he endorsed or suspended for reckless driving or certain other offences. The highest speed at which a car may be driven on the public highways is 20 miles an hour, which is reduced to 10 miles under special local regula- tions. It is an offence under the Motor Car Act (1903), to drive a car in such a way on a public highway as to be dargerous to the public, having regard to all the circumstances of the case. In the event of an accident to any person due to the presence of the motor car on the road, the driver must supply his name and address, and the owner and number of the car, under heavy penal- ties. The driver must produce his licence at the request of a police constable.
