BETTING PROSECUTION.

£s:Fine and a Warning for the

Future.

At the Warrington Police Court on Monday, before the Mayor (Alderman Smethurst) and other magistrates, Thomas Drew, wiredrawer, 34, Leicester-street, was summoned for frequenting Quay-fold for the purpose of betting. Mr. Steel, solicitor, appeared for the defence. P.C. Woollacott stated that on June 9th he and P.C. IHolland secreted themselves in a building in Quay-fold. They bad a good view of the whole of the fold. At 1.15 the defendant appeared on a bicycle from the ' direction of Liverpcol-road. He went to a house where-an old man and woman lived and handed over what appeared to be money. At 1.25 defendant went over the railway croesing and received three slips, which appeared to contain money, from three different men. At 1.30 defendant began a con- versation with the signalman in the signal box. He had his hands behind his back and a youth came up and placed a slip and a silver coin in them. Witness was then about ten Tanis away. Defendant was arrested and brought to the Police Station. He was searched and Es. in silver and 2,id. was found on him. There were no slips. Witness had previously seen defendant loitering in Quay Fold, and also in Leicester-street. P.C. Holland corroborated. On the way to the Police Station the defendant asked witness: " Have you seen me take any bets?" and witness replied, " Yes." Defendant raid, "Well, I admit taking the last bet at the crossing." %Then Drew was searched at the Police Station they found Es. in silver and 20. in copper on him. He made a voluntary statement to the effect that if he had known he had to be searched he would have put ss. out of the Bs. in witness's pocket to make the money look lees. Mr. Steel :_ That was a bit of gammon, melt not?

• Witness : I cannot say, but he -seemed. to take the thing seriously. Mr. Steel said he was going to make a bold statement and leave the case there. It was this—that the police had failed to prove their case. It was for the prosecu- tion to make out their case. The police officers had said they saw slips of paper and money pass, but it had not been proved that the defendant was at the place named for the purpose of betting. Even if it were true that slips had passed it was no proof that he was there for the purpose of betting. Although the officers bad the man in view the whole of the time no betting slips were found on him. _ _ _

The magistrates retired from the Court to consider their decision, and on their return in about five minutes. The Mayor said: The decision of the Bench is that the ease has been proved. We have thought the matter over very carefully, and we are going to inflict what in the opinion of some of the magistrates is a light sentence—£s and costs. There was a division on the Bench, and the amount might have been much more. If the defendant comes here again the amount will not be a. The Bench are determined to put down this gaming.

LETTERS TO THE EDITOR.

-NOTIOE TO CORRESPONDENTS. Authors of letters intended for publication most in all cases send their names and addresses to the Editor as a guarantee of good faith.

Warrington Children's Summer Camo. I . Sir,—The time for sending the children to camp is near at hand. It is necessary to make provision for games. I should be exceedingly obliged if any friends having spare cricket bats, bills, stumps, old footballs, tennis balls, skipping ropes, would kindly send them on to ma for the children's use. Thanking all kind donors in anticipation.—Yonrs,etc., W. H. J. JENKINS, lion. Sec. Bank Chamber, Warrington. uth June, 190 S.

Re Efficiency of the Technical School. Sir,—l should like to add to my last letter OD the above subject, a suggestion that in connection with money allowed for the up-keep of the Main Laboratory an allowance of at least .Q.") per annum should be made for the purchase of standard and modern works on the subjects taught, and to be available for the students. The students hero are very heavily handicapped in this line, on account of the price- of such books, and when it is considered that the majority of the students attend- ing the Institute intend to make a living at the sub- jects they take at the evening classes, such books should be provided either at the Technical School or else at the Municipal Library. A brief study of the books at the above Library, will soon convince anyone as to tie shortage of literature on such subjects as Chemistry, Physics, Electricity, Engineering, and allied subjects. The preponderance of books on Natural Science (a subject, which in any of its branches, is not made a living of by one person in a thousand) over those on the above mentioned subjects, is utterly ridiculous, when so many young fellows are taking up those sciences (Chemistry, Engineering, etc.) for a living and profession. It may be argued against this, that those books already in the Lending Library are very rarely called for, and hence it is not necessary to purchase new ones. Why is it so? Because those books are, with a few exceptions, out of date,aml not in accordance with Izoclern theories.—Yours, etc., A CHEMICAL STUDENT.

An Appeal from the Bishop of Liverpool Sir,—Will you allow me to bring the needs and claims of our Temperance Society through your paper before the general public? For many years past, the gradual increase in the Society's work nod usefulness has been greater than the increase of our income, with the result that there is an animal deficit of something like £5OO. In the year 1905 a very successful Bazaar in St. George's Hall relieved us of a burden of debt and helped to carry on the work up to the present time. Now we are drifting into debt again, and an earnest effort must at once bo made to prevent the beneficent operations of the Society from being hindered or crippled. As most of your readers know, our agents and missionaries are striving to promote the great cause of Temperance, to help to raise up men and women who are falling, or who have fallen, into crime, to provide honest employment for those who have lost work and character, and to protect the young from the evils of intemperance. May I ask those who read this appeal kindly to lend a helping hand by sending donations or mama sub- scriptions (however small) to the Diocesan Secretary (Rev. P. IV. Pheysey) at the Society's offices, B. Com- merce Court, 11, Lord-street, Liverpool.—Yours faithfully

F. J. LIVERPOOL (President of the Diocesan C.E.T.S.) The Palace., Abercrornby-so,uare, Liverpool, 2.3 th June, 1903.

MR. J. A. SEDDON, M.P. _ A Startling Rumour Contradicted.

The London correspondent of the "Manchester Cuardian,"in Thursday's issue,published the following: I hear that Mr. Seddon, the Labour moruber for the 'Newton division. is being pressed to stand at the next election in the interests of his party for Orkney and Shetland. Whether Mr. Seddon will yield to the request is doubtful, but it is his inten- tion in the autumn to address a series of eight meetings in the constituency. This appeared to us such startling news for Mr. Seddon's constituents in the neighbouring Newton Division that we wired him at the House of Commons as follows : . . ' • •

" What about the Orkneys. Any statement to make." itr.Seddon's reply came promptly as follows: "Contradict report. No intention of leaving Newton. Only speaking for party." This is re-assuring, for Mr. Seddon has made him- self too popular and capable a representative to be readily spared from the Newton Divison.

===== 0002601_19080627_art0266.txt | included=true | included =====
ROYAL COURT THEATRE.

Mr. Ryder Boys' Company has deserved a larger measure of public support than has been given to it this week. The company is in every way capable and competent and one can only account for the sparse audiences by the theory that the out-of-door conditions of nice midsummer weather are just now too strong a counter attraction. The company plays the comedy "Sophia " adapted from Fielding's Tom Jones " in an all-round satisfactory and well-balanced way. The adaptation itself is not beyond reproach in the fact that some of the dialogues, such as the one occurring between mistress and maid quite early in the piece, are tediously long drawn cut, but the acting of one and all concerned in the play was uniformly good. The two representative English gentlemen of the period in which the action moves— Squire Western and Mr. Allwortby— were presented Isy Mr. Ryder H,ys and Mr. William Calvert in that jovial "bail fellow well met " style which has mane a long way to the building up of our conception of the ideal " John Bull," while the contrasted characters of Tom Jones and Nephew Blifd were worthily impersonated by Mr. Leonard Mudie and Mr. Henry Worrell. Of the two other people in the play who are closely associated, Tutor Square and Aunt Tabitha, it may truthfully be said that the representaticns of these by Mr. Harold Neville and Miss Florrie flicks were so good that one alnacst forgot that they are but secendary characters in the play. The other minor pelts of Partridge and the poacher Seagrin, especially the first mentioned, were also well dune by Mr. George H. Pye and Mr. Archie Forbes. We have not left the four important Parts of "Sophia," " Lady Bellaston," " Mistress tionour " and "Molly Seagrin " for our last word because the acting was, in these cases, other than fully equal to that of the other people in the comedy. "Sophia Western" was indeed played by Miss Gladys Morris with a naturalness and a modesty which were very becoming, and it was all the more praiseworthy inasmuch as the. difficulty of portraying the manners, and especially the etiquette, of a byegone period, must be recognised. The " Mistress Honour " of Miss Betty Noel was an admirable and appropriately , vivacious impersonation while the part of the scheming and manoeuvring Lady Bellaston was most ably undertaken by Miss Ethel Norbury. " Molly Seagrin." is a somewhat thankless part, but Miss Elsie Vron ! played it to the life aud therein proved herself a most capable actress. We can confidently recommend theatre goers to see "Sophia" on the one opportunity (this evening) yet remaining.

NEXT WEEK'S PROGRAMME.

Mr. Ryder Bovs' Company will again be with us nest week and an admirable programme has been arranged. On Monday, Tuesday and Wednesday the . attraction will be the great Nihilist drama "The Red Lamp" as played by Mr. Beerbohm Tree with enor-' mous success. The cast is a powerful one, including Mr. William Calvert as Paul DemetrinsAfr.Calvert has been touring with Mr. William Mollison's Shakespearean Co. and has won golden opinions for his magnificent performances of Ingo and Miteduff. After next week ' he leaves to play the title role in the great produc- tion of " Rob Boy" at Edinburgh, in which Mr. Ben Davies, the famous tenor, also appears. On Thursday, Friday and Saturday the famous drama " The Robbery of the Lyons Mail" will be presented, with Mr. H. Worrall in the great dual role of Dubose and Lesurques. Mr. H. Worrall played Silvius in " As you like it," at the St. James' Theatre, London. He afterwards joined Mr. H. B. Irving on his provincial tour and accompanied him to America. On his return he was specially engaged to play leads with Mr. William Mollison's Shakespearean Co. He has played in " The Lyons Mail" with Mr. H. B. Irving. Our readers are alvised not to miss these splendid productions.

PROGRESSIVE LABOUR LEAGUE.

What the ri averment Has Done.

TARIFF REFORM FALLACIES EXPOSED.

The Warrington Progressive Labour League has this week been conducting an active open-air campaign, public meetings having been held in various parts of the borough. The first meeting took place on Monday evening on the open space opposite St. Paul's Church in Bawsey-road and was well attended. Mr. Joseph Elwell presided and was supported by Mr. Joseph Howes, Mr. E. England, Mr. J. Morton, and Mr. J. Howes, ;RD. (Liberal agent). The CHAIRMAN said the League was -brought into existence with the object of furthering the interests of labour and pi-ogress and of endeavouring to bring to an end a Government whose time was mainly occupied in legislation of a class character which was detrimental to the best interests of the workers. Some of the things which they advocated when they formed the League had now been placed on the Statute book. They agreed with their

Tory Governments. The measures included the Employers Liability Act, bringing inside its operations all that the late Tory measures exchMed, even to the domestic servants ; the Merchant Shipping Act—the first real great measzre of enfranchisement for the British seaman. Perhaps of more importance than the legislative achievements were the achievements in the various departments. There bad never been a Govern- ment which had done so much real work of reform in the departments. TRADE UNIONISM HAD BEEN RECOGNIZED

and encouraged. The postal department was a striking case in point. Whereas under the late Tory Postmaster General the men were insulted, trade unions condemned and ignored, now the men were consulted and trade unionism was established in the department with groat success. The pay of the workers in the postal service had been increased and that had also been done in other departments. Their Tory friends said that at Woolwich and elsewhere a number of men had been dismissed. That was quite true but curiously enough their opponents did not tell thorn that during a similar period a larger number of men were dismissed by the Tory Government. The difference between a Liberal Government and a Tory Government was the difference between daylight and darkness, between real reform and stagnation. The present Liberal Government had 1 decreased the tea and sugar duties, paid off Tory debts, settled our foreign policy and inaugurated a system of ' old-age pensions. He had note word to say against the system of old-age pensions. Ile did not break his heart because it was too small; he gloried because the pensions had commenced, knowing that the possibibility of the increase could go on to any extent. The Tories said: Where can you get the money from ? They need not , disturb themselves about that. They found plenty of money to kill people in South Africa ; the Liberal , Government would find a little to keep people alive in , England. (Applause.) The Tories promised old-age I pensions long enough. They fought two or three elections upon it and the fault the Liberal party had committed was that they had happened to perform it. , (Applause.) The Pension Bill would go through. He did not think the Lords would dare to interfere with it; if they did it would be all the worse for them. The ; Tories said they were going to fight the next election , on Tariff Reform. " I don't know whether it will ; surprise you "continued Mr. Howes "to know that • I svE ARE ALL TARIFF REFORMERS.

The Socialists endeavoured to persuade the people that there was no difference between the Liberals and the Conservatives but he maintained there was a great difference between the policies of the two parties. For instance, the late Government brought Chinese labour into existence. The policy of the present Government had been to stop the importation of the Chinese into South Africa, and ultimately to do away with that labour altogether. The Conservatives raised the cry : Why were not the Chinese banished from South Africa? But if such a course had been attempted compensation would have been claimed. The Liberal Government had shown its regard for the interests of the workers by passing such measures as the Workmen's Compensa- tion Act and the Trades Disputes Act, the latter of which secured the funds of trade unions. Then there was Mr. Lloyd George's Patents Act, the results of which were to be seen in new industries which were springing up in this country. At Widnes they were erecting a works which was a direct outcome of the passing of the Act, and similar good results had accrued at Ellesmere Port and other places along the Mersey. Then there was the Feeding of School Children Act, the Court of Criminal Appeal Act, the Small Holdings Act, and last but not least, the Old Age Pensions Bill. They did not say the measure now before the House was a final effort with regard to pensions, but it was a good beginning. Germany with many years of experience and with all her protection, had only been able to devote something like .V. 2,0000,000 for the benefit of the aged, but the present mixt:sure would make a start with £6,000,000 or .£7,000,000. The great question of Free Trade is not only a financial and pislitical question ; it is a great moral and religious question as well. The human family is one huge family and the great Creator and Provider has always provided enough and to spare for all the human beings in the world. It is only man's inhumanity to man that creates monopolies and corners and interferes with the free distribution of the good things." MEETING IN HOWLEY WARD.

ashamed of him. Instead of voting against measures of reform he bad voted in favour of them, and at the present time Warrington stood for progress and not for political stagnation. Mr. Howes gave an exhaustive review of the work done by the Government at home and abroad. In less than 12 months he hoped they would see the last of the Chinamen in South Africa and he sug ested that there should then be a great ercancipatio day in Warrington, with a procession through the streets and a great meeting at Parr Hall. (Applause.) Mr. Outhwaite had intimated his willing- ness to core on that occasion. Mr. Howes dealt with Mr. Chamberlain's fiscal proposals and said Protection always meant that a few men should be made rich but that the majority should be made poor. At the close questions were invited. One member of the audience asked if Mr. Howes did not consider that Free Trade and Protection had proved themselves failures, in view of the industrial conditions of the workers to-day? a Mr. Howei : No I think Free Trade hes proved glorious success because in this Free Trade country the workers are better off than in any Protected country in the world. What we have to do is not to stay where we are but to march on to greater victories, being prepared at all times to get any improvement we can and go on to further groat developments of our industrial system. A VOICE : Which class is better off, the workers or the shirkers ? Mr. Howes : Poor shirkers are worse off, rich shirkers are better off. The man who shirks, whether he be rich or poor, deserves punishment. The man who works deserves encouragement. The next question asked was : Is it not a fact that If the Labour party had deleted clause 3 from their Unemployed Bill, the Government would have sup- ported it ? Mr. Howles : You mistake yourself when you talk about the Labour party. You generally mean the Socialist party. (" Question.") The Socialist party is not the Labour party. Never make that mistake. A VorCE : There is only one Socialist in the House of Commons, sitting independently as a Socialist. The oth3rs have been seat there by trade unionists and labour men.

Mr. HowF.s That's a clever thing. Thero's only one Socialist la the Hone of _Commons.

The QUESTIONEIt : Thero's only one sitting as a Socialist—Victor Grayson. Mr. HOWES : Is not Kier Hardie? The QUESTIONER : He is a Socialist but he has been sent as a Labour _man,

Mr. HOWFIi : That's a very clever juggle. We have got a Labour party in the House of Commons. Please reniember that the Socialists are not, the Labour party. Every man who is connected with Labour is a member of the Labour party and it is the greatest piece of impudent arrogance for a handful of Socialists to call themselves the Labour party. They never were and never will be—in fact your Socialist movement is getting so aristocratic that it's a question whether labourers will be able to associate with it. What with your countesses and your mining magnates of South Africa who fought Mr. Maddison, you know, 300 can- not claim to be a Labour party. You are getting an eminently aristocratic Socialistic party. The QUESTIONER.: Hyndman is not a Labour man. Mr. flowEs : Oh, they have chucked " Hyndinan now. _ _

• . The QUESTIONER : Ho is a Socialist. Mr. HOWES : That's what I say. The Socialist party is getting so aristocratic that there will be no r oom for Labour men. _ .

The QUESTIONER: Is it not a fact that if the Labour party had consented to delete clause 3 from its Unemployed Bill which said that the State should see that every man who was xvilling and able to work should have the right to work or be provided with the ne3essaries of life, tho Liberal party would have voted for it instead of walking into the division lobby linking the Conservative party. Answer the question. No quibbling. Flows : The Labour party never introduced the Bill.

The QUESTIONER : You didn't. Mr. Howns : The Socialists did, but the Labour party did not. The QUESTIONER. : Answer the question. Mr. HowEs : I say the Labour party never intro- duced it. If the Socialists had deleted the clause, the Bill would have been no good at all and no man with sense would have voted for it. You cannot pass a Bill to compel everybody to have work and provide for those who do not. The thing is too silly. There would be a great many who would not get work then. (Laughter). We do not play at fooling with legislation. We go on and get the measures step by step. A VOICE : What about the by-elections ? Mr. HOWES : At Pudsey the Socialist and his ally the Tory have won a victory against reform. It is a glorious thing to be proud of—that by linking on to the Tory party you stop reform. You are welcome to it, but we shall go on working against you. Socialism is the worst form of Toryism known.

ANOTHER QUESTIONER : Has not John Burns turned traitor to the Labour party ? Mr. HowEs : No. Since he has been in office he has been the greatest friend the Labour party ever had. If you had not had John Burns in that office, you might have had an aristocrat who knew nothing about the battle of life. J ohn Burns is a practical man with a deep sympathy for those with whom he has worked all his life. He is as true a friend to Labour to-day as ever he was, with more power to help them. (Applause.)

MEETING IN ST. JOHN'S WARD.

A meeting was held on Wednesday in Hardy-street and was presided over by Mr. E. England in the absence of Mr. T. Barnes, who eras suffering from a cold. Accompanying the Chairman on tho platform were Messrs. Howes, Morton and Elwell.

Mr. ENGLAND said that under the Conservative regime the national debt was very much increased. They were aware that the tea and sugar duties and income tax were increased and all of them were cer- tainly burdened by taxation, directly or indirectly. The Liberal party had reduced the national debt and decreased taxation in several ways. The sugar duty was lessened ; the duty was taken or! tea ; and the income tax was reduced. The Liberal party had shown that they were superior in managing the fivances of the country. (Applause). Mr. J. ELWELL sail that as a workingman he was always willing to give what he believed to be the best advice to the workingmen for their own welfare. It was very probable that at the next general election the issue would be, like the last one, on Free Trade or Pro- tection. They as progressives believed that no Govern- ment should pander to class legislation ; but its legisla- den should be of such a character as to benefit the whole community. When the last Government was in power a great deal of their time was occupied in passing laws, which in many instances were against the interests of the workers. He referred to the Taff Vale decision, which placed the whole of the Trade Union funds in a very precarious condition. If they went back to the period of the last Government, they would find that the Member for Warrington voted against the interests of the workers in regard to the Trades Disputes Bill. He found that in going thruugh the record of the late Member's career, that on each occasion he had voted on that Bill, it Was in the interests of the employers and not of the workers. He believed that in the present Government they had one that was honest and endeavoured to do its utmost for the benefit of the people of this country. He did not say that they had been successful in doing everything. When the Government had done its duty the Tills had to pass on to that heredi- tary chamber,—the House of Lords. He believed that the hereditary chamber was an absurdity. Mr. Chamber- lain said that it was necessary that they should give a preference to the colonies and to do that they must put a tax upon the necessaries of life. Lot him (Mr. Elwell) as a workingman ask how this would operate. No matter how small the tax, it increased the cost of living. During the time the preference was put on in Canada from 1896 to 1202 our increased trade with them was only 76.2 per cent, but at the same time the American trade with Canada was increased by 61.5 per , cent. America and Canada were next door to each ' other and no preference would ever give us the Canadian market. Canada had herself declared that she was for the Canadians.

Mr. J. HoWES also spoke on Tariff Reform, and at the close be answered several questions. One of them was :—" The Liberal Government being the strongest Government of modern times, why did it not stand for the Unemployed Bill ?" Mr. Howes replying said that the Liberal party voted money for the unemployed soon after they got into power, and there was some money there not yet applied for. The Unemployed Bill was introduced by the Socialistic section of the House of Commons, and it was a bill that no sane man would support. A telegram was received from Mr. A. IL Crosfield, M.P., expressing the hope that the meetings would be a success.

MEETING lh WHITECROSS WARD,

More Tariff Reform Fictions Exposed. The " Ruination" of Colchester.

On Thursday evening a meeting was held on the open space off Elizabeth-street, in ‘Vhitecross Ward. Coun- cillor J. Hawthorn presided and amongst others present were :—Alderman Burton, Councillor Evan; Messrs. J. Howes, J. Elwell, J. Morton and J. Howes, jun. The CHA IRMAN in referring to Government measures affecting the Nrorking classes pointed out that the Workmen's Compensation Act had been extended so as to include thousands of workers whom the Tories left out, and the Trades Disputes Bill had made the funds of trade unions practically impregnable. The Old-Age Pensions Bill as it stood might not be regarded as much, but the important element was that it was a start They were hoping, with the aid of progressive Liberals,. to make the Bill stronger as years went by and as the funds of the country were able to meet increased demands. The panacea for the evils

of the present day, according to the Tories, was tariff I reform. They were talking very strongly about broaden- ing the basis of taxation, which in other words meant that they wished to make a raid on the poor man's cupboard. They wanted to repeat the experiences of the a hungry forties." For the workingclasses to fall into the hands of the Protectionists would be like jumping out of the frying pan into the fire. It would not make things any better for them, but a great deal worse. Protection should therefore be avoided as the plague. The protection the workers wanted was such as was afforded to them by the Liberal and Labour members, which provided them with old-age pensions. They wanted protection from the brewing trade and from unemployment. Those who had robbed the people of their heritage in the land were the very people who at the present time were advo- cating a tax on wheat, and the rich manufac- turers who were hoping to reap a golden harvest from their investments were those who were spending money freely to get a 10 par cent, tax on imparted goods. Liberals maintained that if we were going to progress, the experiment which was tried sixty years ago of remitting all taxation on the importation of wheat must be continued, and in the interests of the people we must maintain Free Trade. (Applause.) Mr. J. ELwEr.r. then spoke on the tariff reform ques- tion. He maintained that the iron trade was not "going" because of the wicked foreigner, but that it was in a state of transition, arid would never Come back to this or any other country unless some cheaper process of manufacture than the labourons method of puddling could be discovered. Mr. Elwell showed how steel had become such a powerful competitor owing to cheapness of production, and pointed out that only our country with Free Trade had bean able to retain its hold on the manufacture of iron.

Mr. HOWES expressed the hope that their opponents would keep to what they called tariff reform because if there was One plank in the Liberal platforni which was stronger than another it was the Free Trade plank. There was no part of their Free Trade policy which could be successfully impeached. Mr. Howes alluded to the methods of the tariff reformers who, he said, were not broad-minded patriots but very little Euglanders, They would pro- pose to alter the whole of the financial arrangements of the empire because in the coraer of some village or town there was some industry which was not as prosper- one as they thought it ought to be. He wanted to call his hearers attention-to a very melancholy column of news which appeared in the Tory paper of this town last week. When he read it, ha felt very uncomfortable but not for long because he knew it was not true. The column related to Colchester and told them what a melancholy condition it was in. That famous town was ruined—so said the Tory newspaper last week."Colchester to say the best " read the article "has just the ordinary quiet trade of a country town. A few years ago it was a busy hive of industry. The air was filled with the hum of machinery until Free Trade,the foster- , parent of the foreign dumper, shut the doors of nearly all the factories." He cut out the article and sent it to Colchester,asking for information as to the facts of the case, and he got it that morning. As he suspected, the article was all false. Colchester to-day was one of the most prosperous towns in the country. It was progressive and go-ahead to a degree that would be welcomed by any large centre of industry. In 1871 its inhabitants numbered 22,000 ; 1331, .23,000 ; 1891, 31,000 ; 1901,38,000 ; 1908, 41,000. The rateable value in 1393 was £143,000. In 1903 it was £173,000. Take the strength of the Co-operative movement in Col- chester, which had grown up entirely under Free Trade. Last year the local society whose capital was £70,009, was doing a trade of between £140,000 and £150,000. The number of inhabited houses had increased from 0,419 in 1891 to 7,771 in 1901 and in 1907 125 new houses were built. Mr.Howes gave some interesting details as to the industries of Colchester. Paxman and Co., engineers, established 1835, were now employing about 1,000 hands and had a hold on the markets of the world. Mumford's, where they employed 100 men 30 years ago, were now employ- ing 300 and had just arranged for extension of premises. Stanford's, a new firm of agricultural implement makers, had 509 men on their weekly wages sheet. F.W. Brackett, ago-ahead new firm, born and living under Free Trade, gave work to a large number of skilled engineers and sold their pumps in every part et the world. The Colchester Lathe Works had just acquired new premises and electrical engineer- ing was also well established in the town. Owen Perry Ltd., London Docks and Colchester, had so increased their factories that at the agricultural show last week they announced that they were putting down plant enabling them to turn out 1,000 tons of oil cake Iper week. The starting of the great business of Marriage and Son in Colchester dated from the Free Trade era of the forties, and was another great industry that had prospered under the Free Trade redints. In the print- ing business there were fewer than 50 persons employed a quarter of a century ago. Now there were 300, and Messrs. Eyre and Spottisasoode were opening works in Colchester which would give employment to an addi- tional 500. Messrs. George's boot business (stated to have been closed) still existed where it had been estab- lished many years, and was the headquarters of a number of other shops which had grown oat of it and were controlled by Mr. George, who still resided in Colchester. Mr. Howes showed that the closing of certain other works was not due to foreign competition and that other busineesel had taken their place. With regard to Asssrs. G. P. Llazell and Co., boot manufac- turers (stated to hays closed), Mr. Hazell retired owing to ill-health and heavy business, which was still being carried on la Colchester by his old employees. Industries changed in Colchester as elsewhere, but for a ruined town the Protectionists had better try again. (Laughter.) Protection, continued Mr. Howes, was always a fraud, which ever way they looked at it. Tariffs were pat on in order that a few men might corner a trade and become rich at the expense of the multitude of the people who by their limited iacomes were compelled to go to the cheapest market to obtain the necessities of life. Tariffs were the designs of monopolists built to divide men from men, bnt Free Trade broke down the artificial barriers and recognised that " a man's a roan for a' that," wherever ha might be born. (Applause.) The only question put to Mr. Howes was: " Don't you think it would be better instead of opening our ports to let wheat come in, to cultivate our preserves and deer forests and so grow a lot of our own ? ' Mr. 'Owns : It is always better to cultivate your land whenever you can, but at tke seine time keep your ports wide open. Then if your crops fail at borne you can get plenty from abroad. A vote of thanks to the Chairman concluded the 'meeting.

===== 0002601_19080627_art0275.txt | included=true | included =====
PALACE AND HIPPODROME,

Though we 'are getting somewhere near the " clog days " the proprietors of the Palace and Hippodrome continue to attract good crowds to their popular house of entertainment. The quality of the shows "is well-maintained, the 'orchestra under Mr. Widdop still plays most acceptably, and the general manage- ment of the place gives satisfaction to patrons. This week much interest has been aroused by the perform- ance of Sahib Gustave Fasola "the Didion Fakir and wonder-worker," who mystifies admiring audiences with various species of magic such as might have cost him his life as a sorcerer in the middle ages. His sleight of hand is wonderful, while the mysteries he produces on the stage are most puzzling. What can you make of a man who visibly shuts himself up in a box with top, sides and bottom in full view, and presently appears among the audience " though the box is apparently as before? Something of the same sort happens with a young lady whom he boxes up similarly, and who is found in another box entirely when he fires a pistol. The Suttcliffe- troupe are also a big draw in the shape of novelty acrobats, pipers and dancers. They give a clever bagpipe " turn," but their feats as acrobats are the i , best port of their entertaininent and never fail to bring clown the house. The Bros. Bradshaw, acrobatic eccentricities, twist , themselves into every conceivable position, and the humorous way in which they go through their per- formance causes roars of laughter. Cissie Lorpino,l speciality dancer, pleases the audience with her , ; excellent dancing. She goes through the Highland !fling, the Irish jig, a Dutch Dance, and several others in an exceedingly able manner. Irene and Morton, in ! a brilliant comedy interlude, "The hobo and the I maid," keep the audience in high glee. Mark Henton ' the mirthful one, convulses the audience with his droll stories. The Lovelaces sing a couple of illustrated songs," Ain't you corning back to old New Hampshire, Molly?" and " Alice, where art thou going?" in excellent style. The Bartholomew girls also provide a very acceptable turn. Raymond's !Ilio-Tableaux projects the race for the Derby and "A Pretty Typist."

-NEXT WEEK'S PROGP.k.MIIE.

Many attractive features are announced for next week. The bill is headed by the great French Alelot, a marvel of deftness in conjuring who produces ! beautiful flowers and excellent cigars from nowhere ! and distributes them amongst the audience. Mr. J. Gar Kiddie and Co. will appear in their screamingly funny absurdity The House of Tinn " which is sure to cause great merriment. Mr. Kiddie will figure as Jeremiah Jinks, the new waiter, the scene of the sketch being a dining-room at Mermaid Nook. The ether artistes will be : —The Varleys, harmonising duettists ; Virto in his refined! drawing-room musical art ; Chassino, the great shadowgraphist ; "Mr. E.," who will pose as a pit- pat patterer and portray piquant points in peculiar pictures ; The Forsyths in their speciality act " The tramp in the nursery" ; The Evalos in jocular ! jugglery, merry manipulating and gymnastic gyrations. MEETING LAST NIGHT. Visit of ik F. Maddisan, ra.P. CRITICISM OF--TilF KING. Victor Grayson " A Political Fraak."

Special interest centred in the closing meeting of the series which was held at Bridge Foot last evening, as the principal speaker was Mr. Fred Maddison,M.P. for Burnley. There was a large attendance, presided over by Mr. 13. Wynne-Jones. There were also present on the platform : —Rev. C. Harvey-Gook, Messrs. F. Maddison, 112., C. E. Parker, J. Howes, J. Elwell, and J. Morton. The CHITIVA:AN said it was quite clear there was a new movement taking place. The people were finding out what a great power they could exert in combina- tion and for the perfecting of the common life. He maintained that the Liberal party stood for all that was bast and noblest in that great new movement. The party had done much to fulfil the promises aud I pledges made at the last election. He did not think I they would abandon that great system of Free Trade for the benefit of the tariff reformers. They were told there was only going to bo a small tax which would be paid by the foreigner. If the foreigner were going to pay he would say let him pay, but to tax the foreigner was absolutely impossible. It never could ba done. If the disease was so great and the remedy certain why not go in for total prohibition altogether ? Tariff reform would not : remedy the evils of unemployment. He did not want to say anything particularly against Socialism. lie . Ibelieved the Socialists had done much good in drawing attention to various evils but ha could not understand , the kind of Socialism represented by the candidates at North,West Manchester and Pudsey. Whilst Liberals had no sympathy with extreme Socialism they were in earnest about social teform. (Applause). • Mr. F. MADDisox, M.P., who hada cordial reception, said that since the great victory at the last general election he had had over two years' experience of their Member's Liberalism and the pleasure of his company iin the House of Commons, and he was delighted to be able to say that he did not regret but was pleased that he did a 'little towards Mr. Crostiell's triumphant return. (Applause). The present Liberal Government had been true to its pledges and active and zealous in the work it was sent to do. He quoted the opinion of Mr. Parker, a Socialist member, "no other two years could be instanced when so much demo- cratic legislation had been placed on the statute book." Therefore continued Mr. Maddison, it was the solemn duty of the workmen of England to see that such a Government at by-elections was net injured, hurt, or weakened by

===== 0002601_19080627_art0278.txt | included=true | included =====
IVREC-1:ING CANDIDATURES

because what they wanted was legislation, and they could not have legislation without a government, and a government could not exist without a majority in the House of Commons. Therefore ho who supported the Government was the best friend of social legislation, and he, no matter whether he called himself advanced or not, who deliberately without the slightest chance to win, helped to hand a seat over to the Tories, was the real enemy of progress. (Applause.) Referring to Chinese labour Mr. Maddison said he was not in favour of cancelling 15,000 licenses which were issued previous to the retirement of the Tory Govern- ment, because they would have had to pay for it, and he thought they had paid enough. He, for one, was against saddling the tax-payer even to get rid of the trick played by the Tory Government. The Liberal Government had not only sent the Chinese back, but they had saved the Rand from a horde of 200,000 Chinese serfs who, but for the agitation, would have been there to-day. The second question the Government had been true about was the maintenance of Free Trade. The Tories said they had gone back on Free Trade because they had passed the Patents Act. The Tories, however, admitted that the Act was a very valuable piece of legislation. Then why did they not pass it ? and if it were a departure from Free Trade why did they not after 1903, when at least a majority of the Tories were Protectionists, force an Act of that kind on Mr. Balfour ? (Hear, hoar.) The measure was not Protec- tion, but commonsense. The Tories told them what could ba done by tinkering tariffs. That was all in the imagination, or at any rate in the future. It was, how- ever, computed that 10,000 ADDITIONST. WORKMEN

would be employed at the end of this year as the result of the Patents Act, and that 225,000,000 worth of foreign •capital, largely German, would have to be transferred to this country. The Government had kept the Free Trade faith and was going to keep it, and nobody could keep it in England but a Liberal Government. At the list election many Free Trade Unionists voted for the Liberals and he had always tried to pay his tribute to those men for putting aside party ties, which were very close and saying " I must put the national welfare before the concerns of my 'party." The duty which Unionist Free Traders recognized at the last election still remained intact, for Free Trade was absolutely in danger outside tho Liberal party. If they once let in a Tory Government, aim would certainly be an attempt to reverse our Free Trade system. ,1 Expressing great regret at Mr. Chamberlain's serious 1 and apparently final illness, Mr. Maddison said that was no platform tag with him. He would sooner have a man wrong in earnest than a man right who was lazy. , It was the indifferent man,slo was neither hot nor cold, 1 that ho detested, the man who never did a wrong thing ,or a right one—he was an abomination. Mr. Maddison showed how Mr. Cham'ierlain's predictions as to decay- lag industries had been falsified and particularly instanced cotton, which was said to be " going," and yet had increased its exports of manufactured goods by 261 millions. If workingmen in lVarrington supported a Unionist Government at the next geaeral election by 'Voting for

===== 0002601_19080627_art0279.txt | included=true | included =====
MR. R. PIERPOINT

they most be prepared for protective taxes on food. Protection could only rest for its foundation upon a protective tax upon bread, meat, dairy produce, and upon the necessaries of life. While Germany under Protection was borrowing to meet current expenses, the Liberal Government in England were paying off nearly .C 50,000,000 of the national debt piled up by the late Tory Government. There was no Government in the universe which had ever dared to do in one scheme as much as the, present British Government had done I with the old age pension scheme, which amounted to seven millions yearly, without a single penny contributed by the working men. (Applause.) Some might ask what paying off the national debt mattered to the workingmen of the country. By pay- ing off that large amount the Government was saving over £1,000,000 per year which would otherwise go to international financiers—the men who did the least for the world—and it could be devoted to schemes of reform. Our

FREE Tr:ADE FINANCE,

had proved itself .q-ti.l.t:e-C;.Pab-le-Wine-e-ting the require- ments of the nation and was fully equal to any reason- able demands which might be made upon it. Alluding to the

===== 0002601_19080627_art0280.txt | included=true | included =====
LICENiING BILL

Mr. Maddison said he did not know one single act of social reform equal to the drastic reform of the licensing law. May did not want to rob the widows and orphans; , he was getting ft hit sick of the cry about widows and orphans which was trotted out by the brewers on their platforms. No account was taken of the widows and I' orphans created by the trade. The two great principles of the Bill wore that a reduction of licences was -necessary in the interests of the public and they wanted •to get control of all licences after fourteen years' time limit. Did they want to give that control to the Liberal party, to the teetotallers or to the Socialists ? Not at all. They wanted to give it to the people so that the nation should have the last word about licences which never belonged to anybody but the community which lent them out for a certain period. (Applause). There was no conftscation ; it was merely asserting the community's right, absolute and oatiro, over the licences. The Government were going to pass the Bill—(applause)—but they would only pass it after a tremendous struggle and they must have the people behind them. Con- , tinning, Mr. Maddison said he understood the Socialists were going to form a Government in a year or two and if he might allude to one incident he very much regretted he would say that a very high personage was trying to help Socialism more than Socialism could I help itself. He referred to the report—and he was assum- ing it was true—of the exclusion of 'Mr. geir Hardie from the Ifing's list of guests from the Windsor Garden Party. He thought it a very undesirable thing to bring the King's name into political affairs at all, but one could not keep it out, if it was possible for a report to appear, without being contradicted, that men were kept from what was to a large extent tiepublic function on account of their political opinions. He (Mr. Maddison) believed Mr. Hardie's policy was an unfortunate and in some respects a dangerous one. He (Mr. Maddison) ' believed that Mr. Hardie had most of his life been a wrecker. He did not like Mr. lfardie's policy or methods , but it was just because he did not like them that he was there to protest against any

===== 0002601_19080627_art0281.txt | included=true | included =====
ATTEMPT OF. IMIE_E: LNG

to condition his invitations according to the politics of his guests. (Applause.) If a man had said some scur- rilous thin. about the King's, individuality, he could understand some step like that being taken, but so far as he knew no such charge could be made against Mr. Keit Hardie. He saw that one of the others excluded was Mr. Victor Grayson. Well, Mr. Grayson was just a political freak. Ile did not treat Parliament seriously. It was to him only a piece of mechanism for running political meetings. Ho went occasionally to Parlia- ment. He condescended to shed upon that assembly the light of his countenance now and amain. Nobody cared whether he was there or not. He was all right to tickle the ears of the groundlings, and one could always fill a house if one could clown well. (Laughter.) (A Voice: "Say it before his face.") Hs was quite ready to do so. He had already told him before his face that the way he had treated poverty and drink was a disgrace, in the House of Commons. They 'could dismiss Mr. Grayson as beneath contempt and not counting, but Mr. Keir Hardie stood, in his (Ur. Maddison's) opinion on a different footing. Another member said to have been excluded was Mr. Arthur Ponsonby, who was private secretary to the late Sir Henry Campbell-Bannerman, a man of strist Integrity and a sound, moderate

Radical. He gave his vote against the visit of the King to the Czar and he had a perfect right to de so. He (Mr. Maddison) voted the other way and also spoke orl it, not because he loved tha Czar. He hated autocranY as much as any man could do. He supported the visit of the King because it was part of a policy which had been sanctioned by the Anglo-Russian agreement CO which Socialists raised no opposition in the House of Gammons. What did it matter about the King's visit after we had concluded a treaty ? He did not bother himself much about what kings did. In this country we were ruled by Parliament and not by the King. But the King had done great service to inter- national peace and he (Mr. Maddison) was there to say that it was a bewildering thing to him and he still regarded it as an incredible thing that se sagacious a man of the world as his Majesty should give the appearance of conditioning his invitations to a garden party by the politics of the person who would otherwise be invited. That sort of thing, if true, was. bad. (Applause.) QUESTIONS. At the close of his speech Mr. Maddison had to' answer quite a shoal of questions. One was : Will Mr Maddison tell us why he is opposed to Socialism ? Mr. MADDrsoN : Socialism is to me thc negation of liberty. It seeks to attain its end and to secure its object by the denial of the rights of private property. As a Radical 1 do the other thing. I try to increase the amount of private property held by individuals, so that there may be nobody who does not own private pro- perty. The Socialist professes to get his views by socialisation so that no man can really say he has any private property. You may let him enjoy it bat it does not exist as a right. After a few remarks by Mr. How, the meeting concluded.
